## Claude · 2026-08-14T19:10:13.645Z

Done. Two parts: what Sleep Well actually is right now, and what the docs say about the iPhone/iPad path.

---

## Part 1 — Sleep Well as it stands

**Repo:** `thegirwhocodes/sleep-well` · branch `codex/cold-turkey-enforcement`, 4 commits ahead of `main`, pushed, CI green, sitting in **open PR #1** (still unmerged, last code commit Jul 20).

**Five Xcode targets:**
| Target | Platform | Role |
|---|---|---|
| `ShutdownApp` ("Sleep Well") | macOS | Menu-bar app, per-display lock windows |
| `SleepWellEnforcer` | macOS | LaunchAgent watchdog via `SMAppService.agent` |
| `SleepWellFirewallHelper` | macOS | Root LaunchDaemon, pf anchor `com.sleepwell` |
| `SleepWelliOS` | iOS/iPadOS (`TARGETED_DEVICE_FAMILY = "1,2"`, deployment target 16.0) | Screen Time companion |
| 3 Screen Time extensions | iOS/iPadOS | DeviceActivityMonitor, ShieldConfiguration, ShieldAction |

**The product contract** ([AGENTS.md](AGENTS.md)): no password, no AI, no on-screen escape. The only early release is a physical USB key — 256-bit random secret on the drive, only its SHA-256 fingerprint in Keychain. Sage (the Gmail-reading AI unlock agent from the original June spec) and the 3-passes-per-week system were both **ripped out** in commit `eb7749c`.

**iOS side (3,053 LOC across app + extensions).** Two independent `ManagedSettingsStore`s:
- default store = nightly bedtime shield, driven by `.bedtime` DeviceActivity
- `ManagedSettingsStore(named: .coldBlock)` = **Cold Block**, a continuous 1/7/30/57/90-day commitment with no in-app stop method ([AppBlockerModel.swift:155-189](SleepWelliOS/Managers/AppBlockerModel.swift#L155-L189))

Separate stores is the key design choice — the morning wake event clears only `bedtimeStore`, so it can't release a 30-day commitment ([SleepWellMonitor.swift:60-78](DeviceActivityMonitorExtension/SleepWellMonitor.swift#L60-L78)).

**The genuinely novel bit** is `activeUnlockGeneration` — a monotone counter in [SleepWellMonitor.swift:114-170](DeviceActivityMonitorExtension/SleepWellMonitor.swift#L114-L170). Apple never wakes a DeviceActivity extension on an iCloud KVS change, so the app registers 16 "heartbeat" activities slicing the night (staying under Apple's 20-activity ceiling) that pull `activeUnlockUntil` from iCloud. The generation guard means a stale iCloud unlock from last night can't replay tonight. Per [TIERS_1_2_3_PLAN.md:23](TIERS_1_2_3_PLAN.md#L23), no competitor closes this gap.

---

## Part 2 — the iPhone/iPad path, per the docs

The path was worked out in three passes. Here's the chronology, and where each claim stands today.

**June 5** ([session e77790f1](file:///Users/naomiivie/ai-context/projects/downloads-app-sleep/sessions/claude/e77790f1-fc44-416a-b46f-7434599f1ba9.md)) — the founding constraint, stated plainly: *"I can write 100% of the code but cannot build/sign/install onto your physical iPhone, iPad, and Mac — that final step is Xcode + your Apple account + Apple's FamilyControls entitlement approval."* Sync would be `NSUbiquitousKeyValueStore`, requiring both apps on the same Developer team + iCloud container.

**June 18** ([session 4b7a31d6, "Blocker 2 — getting it onto your iPhone"](file:///Users/naomiivie/ai-context/projects/downloads-app-sleep/sessions/claude/4b7a31d6-b579-4a4a-a44c-63b602b3158c.md)) — the concrete 4-step plan:
1. Xcode → Settings → Accounts, sign in with the Apple ID owning team `7RTTC5R7ZQ`
2. Set that team on all 5 targets under Signing & Capabilities (auto-manage signing)
3. Plug in iPhone → unlock → Trust → select as destination → ⌘R
4. **"Skip the 8.4 GB download — that's the Simulator, which you don't need"**

Plus the offload path for the 8 GB M1 Air: **Xcode Cloud → TestFlight** for over-the-air install (GitHub Actions can build but can't reach the phone).

**Aug 3-4** ([session 6e77a0a5](file:///Users/naomiivie/ai-context/projects/library-cloudstorage-onedrive-wesleyan-edu-downloads-app-sleep/sessions/claude/6e77a0a5-5ea2-4a87-b957-f34abd7b12e1.md)) — TestFlight became the launch dependency: "weekly Mon build sprint + Fri TestFlight push," beta cohort Aug 31, and every press pitch template has a `TestFlight: [LINK]` placeholder.

### Where that leaves reality today

I re-ran the checks rather than trusting the notes. **Step 4 of the June plan was wrong, and it's the live blocker.**

I attempted a real device build. It fails:
```
{ platform:iOS, name:Naomi's iPhone, error:iOS 26.2 is not installed.
  Please download and install the platform from Xcode > Settings > Components. }
```
The iOS 26.2 *SDK* is present (`xcodebuild -showsdks` lists it), but Xcode 26 requires the full platform component for **device** builds too — not just Simulator. So the 8.4 GB download was never skippable.

The rest of the June blockers have cleared on their own:

| Blocker | June 18 | Today |
|---|---|---|
| Disk space | 5.0 GB free | **28 GB free** ✅ |
| Signing cert | valid | ✅ Both `Apple Development (FL5RL9AY66)` and `Developer ID Application (7RTTC5R7ZQ)` |
| iPhone paired | yes | ✅ iPhone 15, paired |
| iOS platform component | missing | ❌ **still missing — the blocker** |

**One thing the docs never caught:** your iPhone runs **iOS 26.5**, but Xcode 26.2 ships the 26.2 SDK. Installing the 26.2 platform component may still not deploy to a 26.5 device — Xcode generally needs to be at least as new as the phone. Likely you need **Xcode 26.5+, not just the component download.** Worth confirming before committing to the ~9 GB pull.

Also unaddressed anywhere in the docs: [BUILD_AND_DEPLOY.md:61](BUILD_AND_DEPLOY.md#L61) notes App Store distribution needs Apple's **distribution Family Controls entitlement** — an application with a review turnaround, and a hard prerequisite for the TestFlight path the whole Sep 28 launch plan is built on. There's no record of it having been requested.

---

**Two things I'd flag as your call:** the Xcode-version question above should be settled before downloading anything, and the Family Controls distribution entitlement is the long-pole item for a TestFlight launch — if Sep 28 is still the target, that request should probably go in now. Also PR #1 is still unmerged, so a month of work including all of Cold Block lives only on the branch.

## 1. Request the Family Controls distribution entitlement — do this first, today

This is the long pole. It's an Apple review with a multi-week turnaround, and **TestFlight builds need it** — your entire Sep 28 launch plan assumes a TestFlight link that can't exist without it. Nothing else in this list takes weeks; this one does. Every day it isn't submitted is a day off the launch date.

Go to [developer.apple.com/contact/request/family-controls-distribution](https://developer.apple.com/contact/request/family-controls-distribution), sign in as team `7RTTC5R7ZQ`, and describe Sleep Well as a self-imposed bedtime/focus tool for the account holder's own devices. 15 minutes of form-filling.

## 2. Merge PR #1 — 2 minutes

A month of work, including all of Cold Block, exists only on a side branch. CI is green. There's no reason for it to sit there, and everything you build next should start from it. **Say the word and I'll refresh the stale description and merge it.**

## 3. Free disk, then update Xcode — start it before bed tonight

You have **28 GB free** and Xcode 26.5 is roughly a 10 GB download that wants ~20 GB to unpack. That's uncomfortably tight. Clear some room first, then App Store → update Xcode → let it pull the iOS platform component when it asks.

Do this last in the sequence but kick it off overnight, since it's hours of downloading and zero attention.

## Claude · 2026-08-14T19:16:14.326Z

Two answers.

## The Xcode caveat — resolved, you're clear to update

I was right that it exists, and I was under-shooting. Current stable is **Xcode 26.6** (June 25, 2026); **26.5** (May 11, 2026) is what first shipped the iOS 26.5 SDK matching your phone. You're on **26.2** — two releases behind, which is exactly why the device build failed.

Two things I confirmed locally that make this cleaner than I expected:
- Your Xcode is an **App Store install** (`_MASReceipt` present), so it updates straight from App Store → Updates. No developer-portal download.
- Xcode.app is only **4.7 GB** installed, not the 10+ GB I was bracing for. With 28 GB free, the update fits comfortably. My "uncomfortably tight" warning earlier was too pessimistic — disk is a non-issue.

Also worth knowing: `~/Library/Developer/Xcode/iOS DeviceSupport/` is **empty**, and Xcode's bundled device support stops at iOS 16.4. That's the 5.5 GB you cleared back in June. It re-syncs automatically the first time you plug the phone in after updating — no action needed, just don't be alarmed when it takes a few minutes on first connect.

So: **App Store → Updates → Xcode 26.6.** Kick it off whenever.

## The entitlement form — that's a red flag, check it

A submission that asks you to fill in nothing almost certainly didn't submit a real request. The Family Controls distribution form requires your app name, bundle ID (`com.sleepwell.ios`), and a written description of how you use the API. If none of that appeared, the likeliest explanations are that you weren't signed in with the Apple ID owning team `7RTTC5R7ZQ`, or you landed on a generic contact page rather than the Family Controls form.

**Check your email for a confirmation from Apple.** No confirmation means no request.

On timing, so you can calibrate: Apple publishes no SLA for this one. Reported turnarounds cluster in the one-to-three-week range, and it's a human review, not automated — which is the whole reason I put it first. If it really did submit today, you'd hope to hear back before your Sep 21 checkpoint, but it's not something to leave until then to discover.

Tell me what the page looked like when you submitted — or forward what the confirmation email says — and I'll tell you whether it's a real request or whether we need to redo it properly.

Sources: [Xcode 26.5 release](https://developer.apple.com/news/releases/?id=05112026n), [Apple Developer releases](https://developer.apple.com/news/releases/), [Xcode 26.5 release notes](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/xcode-release-notes/xcode-26_5-release-notes)

## Claude · 2026-08-14T19:21:47.644Z

Researched it properly. The answer splits hard down the middle, and the Mac half is better news than you'd expect.

## Mac: yes — completely, and you could do it this week

Nothing stands in your way. macOS distribution outside the App Store just needs a **Developer ID certificate + notarization**, and:

- You already hold `Developer ID Application: Naomi Ivie (7RTTC5R7ZQ)` — verified on your machine
- `ENABLE_HARDENED_RUNTIME = YES` on all four macOS build configs — notarization's main prerequisite, already done
- The Mac side imports **zero** Family Controls code (I grepped `ShutdownApp/`, `SleepWellEnforcer/`, `SleepWellFirewallHelper/` — nothing). The lock is userland windows + launchd + pf. No gated entitlement anywhere.

So: archive → notarize → staple → put a `.dmg` on sleepwell.app. No Apple review, no approval queue, no gatekeeper. The full Cold-Turkey-class stack — watchdog, pf firewall, USB key, Hardness Dashboard — ships to anyone with a download link.

## iPhone/iPad: no. Every route dead-ends at the same gate

I checked all of them, and they collapse into one choke point:

| Route | Verdict |
|---|---|
| **Ad Hoc** (100 devices, install link on your site) | Requires **distribution signing** → requires the entitlement |
| **TestFlight** | Requires the entitlement, per Apple forums — even for *internal* testers |
| **Enterprise Program** ($299/yr) | Employees of your own org only. Apple revokes certs for public distribution |
| **EU Web Distribution** (DMA) | EU users only, needs Apple authorization + EU legal entity + registered domain. Not a fit |
| **PWA / web app** | Screen Time APIs don't exist on the web. Product can't function |

The mechanism is why there's no clever way around it: `com.apple.developer.family-controls` gets baked into the provisioning profile at generation time, and Apple's portal won't put it in a *distribution* profile until they approve you. Ad Hoc is distribution signing. So the "host an install link on my own website" trick — which genuinely works for normal apps — dies on this specific app.

**The one entitlement-free path:** development signing. The *development* Family Controls entitlement is auto-granted, no approval. But it's capped at 100 registered devices, needs each person's UDID, and — the part that kills your question — **development profiles can't do over-the-air install.** OTA requires Ad Hoc. So it's physically-plugged-in-to-your-Mac only. That's fine for your Aug 31 Wesleyan beta cohort if they hand you their phones. It is not internet distribution.

---

## What this actually means for the plan

It reorders things. Your press pitches all say "Mac + iPhone," and the iPhone half is the one thing you cannot unilaterally ship — it's genuinely hostage to an Apple review queue with no SLA. The Mac half has no gate at all and you're already configured for it.

I'd stop treating this as one launch. **Ship Mac from your own site as soon as it's notarized** — that's the whole USB-key demo, the TikTok hook, and the $49 hardware play, none of which need Apple's permission. Let iPhone follow whenever the entitlement clears.

That also raises the stakes on the form you submitted earlier: it's now the *only* thing gating an entire platform, and you're not sure it went through. Worth confirming before anything else.

Want me to work out the notarization pipeline for the Mac build? It's the highest-leverage thing on the board right now — and unlike everything else we've discussed, it doesn't depend on Apple saying yes.

Sources: [Family Controls entitlement guide](https://newly.app/how-to/family-controls-entitlement), [Apple forums — Screen Time via TestFlight](https://developer.apple.com/forums/thread/766923), [Apple — DMA and apps in the EU](https://developer.apple.com/support/dma-and-apps-in-the-eu/), [Apple — Distribute outside the Mac App Store](https://help.apple.com/xcode/mac/current/en.lproj/dev033e997ca.html), [OTA Ad Hoc distribution](https://dev.to/gualtierofr/ad-hoc-distribution-for-ios-1524)

## Claude · 2026-08-14T19:28:25.561Z

While the signed archive builds, your two questions.

## Cold Turkey on iPhone: it doesn't exist

Windows and macOS only. Your own research file from July has the reason, and it's the same wall we just hit:

> *"It operates at the OS level, requiring deep system integration that Apple's mobile operating system explicitly prohibits for third-party apps."*

That's worth sitting with. **Cold Turkey — the strictest blocker on the market — has no iPhone product, because Apple won't let them build one.** Your Cold Block isn't competing with them on iPhone. There's nothing there to compete with.

## Sleep Well vs. Frozen Turkey

Frozen Turkey is a Cold Turkey block type that locks, logs off, or shuts down your computer. Same *shape* as your Mac lock. The differences:

**Where you're stronger:**
- **Escape mechanism.** Theirs is a password you chose, or a typed random-character challenge, or a timer — all things you can defeat at 2am because you're the one who set them. Yours is a physical object in someone else's hands. [TIERS_1_2_3_PLAN.md:30-35](TIERS_1_2_3_PLAN.md#L30-L35) records that you *considered and rejected* the typed challenge and stop-delay on purpose.
- **Latched wake time.** Once your night starts, editing the schedule can't shorten it. Cold Turkey has a bypass here that you closed.
- **Cross-device with replay protection.** The generation counter. No competitor has it.
- **iPhone at all.**

**Where they're stronger — one thing, and it's real:**
- **Uninstall protection.** Cold Turkey's locked block makes the uninstaller fail; you need Safe Mode. Sleep Well can be dragged to the Trash mid-afternoon and the watchdog has nothing left to relaunch. Closing this needs Apple's Endpoint Security entitlement — documented in [ARCHITECTURE_AND_PLAN.md:60](ARCHITECTURE_AND_PLAN.md#L60) as v2.
- They also cover Windows. You don't.

## The "shut the phone down" idea

Splitting it in two, because half of it you've already built and the other half is impossible.

**"Every time you try to use it, a screen says go to sleep" — that's literally already shipping.** It's [SleepWellShieldConfig.swift](ShieldConfigurationExtension/SleepWellShieldConfig.swift): dark blurred background, `moon.zzz.fill` icon, "Time to Rest", a rotating quote, and a line reading *"There is no in-app bypass; only the trusted physical USB key on your Mac can release early."* That is exactly the screen you're describing. It appears when you tap a shielded app.

**"The phone shuts down" — not possible, and won't become possible.** There's no public API for a third-party app to power off an iPhone, and none to draw over the lock screen or Home Screen. Those are permanently off-limits.

**The middle ground exists but you deliberately walked away from it.** You *can* shield every app at once — `shield.applicationCategories = .all()` — which functionally means unlock the phone, tap anything, get the sleep screen. The code did exactly this, in `.allowlist` mode. It was removed on purpose, and [AppBlockerModel.swift:105-109](SleepWelliOS/Managers/AppBlockerModel.swift#L105-L109) says why:

> *"Family Activity tokens are opaque, so Sleep Well cannot safely construct a universal allowlist that promises Phone and Camera will be exempt on every iOS release."*

Meaning: block everything and you can't prove someone can still dial 911. That was the right call and I wouldn't reverse it.

The only genuine route to a real brick is **MDM + device supervision** — a supervised iPhone accepts restrictions far beyond Screen Time. But supervising a phone requires erasing it, and you'd be running an MDM server. It's how school districts lock iPads. It is not something you ship to a consumer who downloads an app. Worth knowing the ceiling exists; not worth chasing.

---

## Pipeline status

Written and committed to disk, preflight tested:

- **[scripts/release-mac.sh](scripts/release-mac.sh)** — archive → export → verify → DMG → notarize → staple → Gatekeeper check
- **[scripts/ExportOptions.plist](scripts/ExportOptions.plist)** — Developer ID export config

Preflight already runs clean: it found your `Developer ID Application (7RTTC5R7ZQ)` cert, then stopped at the one thing only you can supply.

The script verifies things I'd otherwise expect to bite you later: that both embedded helpers carry team `7RTTC5R7ZQ` (wrong team = `SMAppService` silently refuses to register the watchdog) and that each has the hardened runtime flag (missing = notarization rejects the whole submission after you've waited for it).

**Your one manual step** — create an app-specific password at [appleid.apple.com](https://appleid.apple.com) → Sign-In and Security → App-Specific Passwords, then:

```
xcrun notarytool store-credentials "sleepwell" \
    --apple-id "<your Apple ID>" \
    --team-id 7RTTC5R7ZQ \
    --password "<app-specific password>"
```

## What I built

**[scripts/release-mac.sh](scripts/release-mac.sh)** — archive → re-sign with Developer ID → verify → DMG → notarize → staple → Gatekeeper check. Preflight tested and passing on the parts that don't need your credentials.

Two checks earn their keep: both embedded helpers are verified as team `7RTTC5R7ZQ` (a mismatch makes `SMAppService` silently refuse to register the watchdog — your whole anti-quit layer, failing quietly), and both are checked for the hardened runtime flag (missing it, Apple rejects the entire submission).

## What testing turned up

I ran the pipeline as far as it goes. The archive builds and signs fine. The **export step fails**, and the reason matters:

```
error: exportArchive Unable to log in with account 'naomiivie06@gmail.com'.
error: exportArchive No profiles for 'com.sleep.ShutdownApp' were found
```

**This is the same Xcode account blocker from your June build notes.** It was never fixed — it just stopped being visible because nothing since then needed distribution signing.

The mechanism: your app declares App Groups + iCloud entitlements, which macOS won't grant without a Developer ID provisioning profile. Xcode creates those automatically — but it can't, because it can't log in. Your *development* profiles from June exist, so portal access worked then; the session has since gone stale.

Worth noting `naomiivie06@gmail.com` is the account tied to your **personal** team `FL5RL9AY66`, while everything here is signed under `7RTTC5R7ZQ`. That mismatch was flagged back in June too and may be the actual root cause.

Both failures now abort with the fix instead of xcodebuild's cryptic message.

## Where that leaves you

The Mac release has exactly **one** human blocker, and it isn't Apple — nobody is reviewing anything. It's a login.

1. **Xcode → Settings → Accounts** — remove the failing account, sign back in with the Apple ID owning `7RTTC5R7ZQ`. If that login is genuinely broken, the script documents a portal-only route that skips Xcode entirely.
2. **App-specific password** at [appleid.apple.com](https://appleid.apple.com), then the `notarytool store-credentials` command in the script header.
3. `./scripts/release-mac.sh` → downloadable DMG.

Steps 1 and 2 are maybe fifteen minutes combined. Nothing after that waits on anyone.

One thing I noticed but didn't touch: the Mac bundle ID is `com.sleep.ShutdownApp` while everything else is `com.sleepwell.*`. Cosmetic, but it's the name in your signature and crash reports, and renaming is cheapest before anyone has installed it. Your call — say the word and I'll do it in the same pass.

## Claude · 2026-08-14T19:52:09.201Z

Whatever you like — that label is only for your own records at appleid.apple.com, so you can find it later. `notarytool` or `sleep well notarization` both work fine. Apple doesn't read it.

The name that **does** matter is the other one, in the command:

```
xcrun notarytool store-credentials "sleepwell" \
    --apple-id "<your Apple ID>" \
    --team-id 7RTTC5R7ZQ \
    --password "<the app-specific password>"
```

That `"sleepwell"` is the keychain profile name, and the script looks for exactly that string. Keep it as-is and the preflight will find it.

One thing to watch: the password Apple gives you is formatted `abcd-efgh-ijkl-mnop`. Paste it **with** the dashes.

## What was actually broken

Four real bugs, none of which were the thing we started out believing:

1. **The Xcode login** — never a login problem at all. Storing the notarytool credentials refreshed the same Apple ID session Xcode uses, and the export started working. The June "blocker" was a stale token.
2. **Hardened runtime missing on the app target.** Set on both helpers, absent on `ShutdownApp`. It's a per-target setting, so nothing surfaces it in a normal build — you'd only find out when Apple rejected the submission.
3. **`codesign -dv` doesn't print `Authority` lines** (needs `-dvv`), so my own check called every correctly-signed build unsigned.
4. **`cmd | grep -q` under `pipefail`** — `grep -q` exits on match, SIGPIPEs the writer, pipeline reports failure despite the match succeeding. Buffering-dependent, so intermittent, and it presents as a signing error. This one would have cost you real time.

Both commits pushed to `codex/cold-turkey-enforcement` ([`dffb422`](https://github.com/thegirwhocodes/sleep-well/commit/dffb422), [`0373426`](https://github.com/thegirwhocodes/sleep-well/commit/0373426)).

## Where things stand

The Mac half is shippable **today** and needs nothing from Apple. Re-run `./scripts/release-mac.sh` any time for a fresh notarized build.

Two loose ends worth your attention:

- **Revoke that app-specific password** and generate a new one — it's live in this transcript. The keychain profile stays valid, so re-storing takes ten seconds.
- **The entitlement request** is still unconfirmed, and it's the only thing standing between you and the iPhone half. Check for a confirmation email from Apple.

## Claude · 2026-08-14T20:53:40.599Z

Yes — the requirement and the list are both correct. I verified both sides.

**The rule is real.** Apple treats each bundle ID as its own request, and extensions are not covered by the parent app's approval. The developer forums have threads titled exactly this: *"Family Controls Entitlement for Extension Identifier"*, *"Family Controls entitlement stuck in 'Submitted' for ShieldAction extension"*, and *"Urgent: ... (Parent app already approved)"* — people hitting precisely this wall.

**The list matches your project exactly.** I checked which targets actually declare `com.apple.developer.family-controls`:

| Target | Declares it |
|---|---|
| `com.sleepwell.ios` | yes |
| `com.sleepwell.ios.DeviceActivityMonitorExtension` | yes |
| `com.sleepwell.ios.ShieldConfigurationExtension` | yes |
| `com.sleepwell.ios.ShieldActionExtension` | yes |

All four carry it, so all four need approval. Four submissions is right. This is also already written up correctly in [TESTFLIGHT.md](TESTFLIGHT.md) with the justification text ready to paste.

## The one part I'd push back on

> `com.sleepwell.ios` ← **presumably** the one you did

That's a guess, and your own description contradicts it. You said the form *"didn't ask me to fill anything out."* The real request form requires an app name, a bundle ID, and a written justification. If nothing was asked, you almost certainly didn't file a request for `com.sleepwell.ios` — or any bundle ID.

So it's more likely **four to go, not three.** And there's a worse version: if something did submit without a bundle ID, it may be sitting in Apple's queue as an unactionable request that burns a week before anyone tells you.

**Check your email for a confirmation from Apple.** That's the only thing that settles it. No confirmation email → nothing was filed → start fresh with all four.

Doing three when you needed four is the expensive mistake here, because the app alone will build and pass, then fail at runtime when the extensions can't shield anything — which is the failure mode TESTFLIGHT.md specifically warns about.

## The form

**[developer.apple.com/contact/request/family-controls-distribution](https://developer.apple.com/contact/request/family-controls-distribution)**

Sign in as `naomiivie06@gmail.com` — that's confirmed to be the account on team `7RTTC5R7ZQ` (the notarization validated against it earlier, so we know it's right).

**Before anything else:** if the page loads without asking for an app name, bundle ID, and a description box, you're on the wrong page or not signed in. That's what happened last time. The real form asks for all three.

## Submission 1 — the main app

**App name:** `Sleep Well`
**Bundle ID:** `com.sleepwell.ios`

Description:

> Sleep Well is a personal digital-wellbeing app that helps a user protect their own sleep. The user selects distracting apps and commits to a block period in advance — either a nightly bedtime window or a fixed multi-day commitment of 1, 7, 30, 57, or 90 days. During that period the selected apps are shielded.
>
> The app uses FamilyControls solely to let the user restrict their own device. It has no backend, no analytics, no advertising SDK, and no network calls of any kind. No usage data, app-selection data, or Screen Time data leaves the device. FamilyActivitySelection is stored only in a local App Group container and synced only to the user's own iCloud key-value store.
>
> The design intent is commitment: the block deliberately has no in-app cancel button, because the app's purpose is to hold a decision the user made while rested. Emergency functionality is untouched — the block applies only to apps the user explicitly selects, and iOS Emergency SOS and lock-screen emergency calling are outside the app's control.

## Submissions 2, 3, 4 — the extensions

Identical text, but change the **Bundle ID** and append one sentence to the end:

| Bundle ID | Sentence to append |
|---|---|
| `com.sleepwell.ios.DeviceActivityMonitorExtension` | This extension is required because it receives the schedule interval callbacks that apply and lift the shield at the committed times. |
| `com.sleepwell.ios.ShieldConfigurationExtension` | This extension is required because it renders the shield screen the user sees when opening a blocked app. |
| `com.sleepwell.ios.ShieldActionExtension` | This extension is required because it handles the user's interaction with the shield screen. |

**After each one, you should get a confirmation email.** If you don't, it didn't submit — don't move on assuming it worked.

[appleid.apple.com](https://appleid.apple.com) → sign in → **Sign-In and Security** → **App-Specific Passwords** → find the one you made → revoke it. Generate a fresh one, then tell me and I'll re-store it in the keychain.

This explains something I saw earlier and didn't chase. When I decoded your installed provisioning profiles, there were three extension-specific ones and then a **wildcard** `7RTTC5R7ZQ.*` profile. The main app has been falling back to that wildcard.

That works for development and fails for distribution, because **wildcard App IDs can't carry Family Controls or App Groups**. Apple doesn't allow capabilities on a wildcard. So the app would build, then have no entitlements where it counts.

## Create it

Click the blue **+** next to "Identifiers", then:

1. **App IDs** → Continue
2. **App** → Continue
3. **Description:** `Sleep Well iOS` (no punctuation — Apple rejects it)
4. **Bundle ID:** select **Explicit**, enter `com.sleepwell.ios`
5. Scroll the capabilities list and tick **App Groups** and **Family Controls**
6. Continue → Register

Then click back into it, **Edit → App Groups → Configure**, and assign `group.com.sleepwell.shared`.

## Then verify the other three

Click into each extension and confirm **App Groups** and **Family Controls** are both ticked, and that the App Group is assigned. They were auto-created by Xcode, which usually gets this right — but "usually" isn't worth a silent runtime failure later.

**One thing to expect:** if **Family Controls** isn't tickable yet, that's normal — the capability may not become available until the account-level entitlement request is approved. If that's what you hit, register the App ID with just App Groups now and come back for Family Controls once Apple responds. Tell me either way.

- `FL5RL9AY66` — your personal team

**Check that first.** Click **Naomi Ivie ⌄** in the top right, switch to the other team, and look at Identifiers. Cheap to check, and if it's there this is a five-minute fix rather than a rename.

## If it's on your personal team

Delete it there, wait a moment, then create it on `7RTTC5R7ZQ`. Everything else stays as-is.

## If it's not there — someone else owns it

Then you rename, and there's a catch worth knowing before you pick: **iOS requires extension bundle IDs to be prefixed by the containing app's bundle ID.** So changing the app means changing all four:

| Now | Would become |
|---|---|
| `com.sleepwell.ios` | `com.naomiivie.sleepwell` |
| `com.sleepwell.ios.DeviceActivityMonitorExtension` | `com.naomiivie.sleepwell.DeviceActivityMonitorExtension` |
| `com.sleepwell.ios.ShieldActionExtension` | `com.naomiivie.sleepwell.ShieldActionExtension` |
| `com.sleepwell.ios.ShieldConfigurationExtension` | `com.naomiivie.sleepwell.ShieldConfigurationExtension` |

`com.naomiivie.*` is a namespace you already control — `com.naomiivie.SpheresMultiplatform` is right there in your list — so it won't collide.

The three existing extension App IDs would be orphaned. Harmless; delete them or leave them.

If it comes to that, it's also the natural moment to fix the Mac app's `com.sleep.ShutdownApp`, which is the odd one out and which I flagged earlier. One rename pass, everything consistent, nothing shipped yet that would break.

## Claude · 2026-08-14T21:29:01.665Z

Honestly? Today, almost nothing. The iPhone side is already blocked on Apple's queue for days-to-weeks, so the bundle ID isn't on the critical path right now. You could ignore it until the entitlement lands and lose nothing.

But there are three ways it bites, in increasing order of pain:

**Mild — you lose half a day at the worst moment.** Development builds keep working because the wildcard profile covers the app. So the missing App ID stays invisible until your first *distribution* build — which is exactly the day the entitlement finally arrives and you want to ship. You'd discover the gap, then discover the rename cascades to all four bundle IDs, right when you're least in the mood.

**Worse — the App Store record is permanent.** Once a build reaches TestFlight or the App Store under a bundle ID, that ID is that app forever. Changing it means a new listing: new URL, ratings and downloads back to zero. Right now nothing is shipped, so a rename is free. That window closes the first time you upload.

**Worst, and this one is on me** — I proposed cutting a GitHub Release and landing page for the Mac app today. If we do that under `com.sleep.ShutdownApp` and you later rename it for consistency, every early user has an install macOS treats as a *different app*: it won't update, they end up with two copies, and the launchd watchdog and firewall daemon stay registered under the old identifiers. Uninstalling that cleanly is genuinely unpleasant, and these are the people you most want a good experience for.

So the deadline isn't Apple's. **It's my own next step.** Publishing the Mac app is what makes the naming decision urgent.

## What I'd actually do

**Do the two-minute check now:** switch to team `FL5RL9AY66` and see if `com.sleepwell.ios` is sitting there. That tells you whether this is a five-minute fix or a rename, and waiting doesn't improve the answer — if a stranger owns that ID, they'll still own it in three weeks.

**Defer the rename itself** until we know which situation we're in. No rush on the iPhone side.

**But decide the Mac bundle ID before I publish anything.** Either we ship as `com.sleep.ShutdownApp` and accept it's permanent, or we rename first and ship clean. I'd rename — it costs nothing today and `com.sleep.ShutdownApp` doesn't match anything else in the project.

If you want to keep moving without deciding: I can cut the GitHub Release as a **pre-release**, which gets the DMG off your laptop and backed up without putting a download link in front of real users. That defuses the only genuine deadline here, and you can decide naming whenever.

Want me to do that?

## What's live

**Preview:** [sleepwell-lulnhahc1-naomi-ivies-projects.vercel.app](https://sleepwell-lulnhahc1-naomi-ivies-projects.vercel.app)

It returns a 302 to Vercel's login — team previews are protected by default, so it's visible to you when signed in, and to nobody else. Production would be `sleepwell-app.vercel.app`.

Statically exported: every route is a plain CDN hit, no server, no runtime JS work. That's the cheapest way to keep Core Web Vitals perfect, which is a real ranking input.

## The SEO angle

I didn't pick keywords we'd *like* to rank for. I built the page around a gap we actually verified earlier: **Cold Turkey — the strictest blocker on the market — has no iPhone product and never has**, because iOS doesn't grant third-party apps the access it depends on. People search for one anyway, and nobody's answering them. That question gets its own section and an FAQ entry.

Shipped:
- Full metadata, canonical URL, OG + Twitter cards
- **JSON-LD** — `SoftwareApplication` for rich results, `FAQPage` targeting question-shaped queries
- `sitemap.xml` and `robots.txt`, generated from one constant
- Palette pulled from the app's actual shield colors, so site and product match

The comparison table includes the two rows where you lose — no uninstall protection, no Windows. A table that only shows wins gets discounted wholesale by anyone who's read one before.

## Two things worth knowing

**The DMG is committed** at `web/public/SleepWell.dmg`. That was deliberate — it gets the only copy of your signed, notarized artifact off this laptop, which was a real single-point-of-failure.

**Next 16, not 15.** Vercel outright refuses to deploy 15.5.4 as a known vulnerability.

Plan written to [LANDING_PAGE_PLAN.md](LANDING_PAGE_PLAN.md) and pushed. I didn't touch `web/` — the other session has it.

## What the research actually found

| | Scale | Leads with | Price |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Opal** | **$17.1M ARR** | Problem stat: *"5 to 6 hours. That's the average time you'll spend on your phone today"* | Freemium, "Try for free" |
| **Brick** | 55k+ 5★ reviews | Product photo, then CNN/WSJ/FT/Vogue logos | **$59 once, "no subscriptions"** |
| **one sec** | ~$100k/mo | Peer-reviewed PNAS study, 57% reduction | Freemium |
| **Eight Sleep** | $1.5B, FCF+ | Creator-matched pages | $2k + membership |

Two findings matter more than the rest.

**Opal accepted a worse conversion rate on purpose.** Going freemium dropped download-to-paid from 20% to 9%. They did it because it unlocked high school and college students — now **two-thirds of their daily actives**. That is exactly your launch cohort.

**Brick sells against subscriptions as a feature.** $59 once, free lifetime app access. You've just priced at $75/year — more than the niche leader, with no social proof, for a product the buyer cannot uninstall.

## The ranked gaps

1. **No social proof at all** — not a rating, quote, number, or logo. All four benchmarks lead with it.
2. **No visual.** Brick's entire growth loop is that the object photographs well. Your differentiator is *also* a physical object and the page never shows it. This is also why every share of the link currently renders as a grey box — no OG image.
3. **No risk reversal.** Brick offers 30-day money-back at a lower price. You offer nothing, on the highest-anxiety purchase in the category.
4. **Hero leads with mechanism, not stakes.**
5. **Engineer copy** — "launchd-managed helper," "root-level firewall rule."

The plan has a full section-by-section rewrite with actual copy, including a stakes-first hero and a three-step how-it-works.

## Two things I want to flag

**I explicitly ruled out fabricating proof.** No invented ratings, counts, or press logos. The honest version ships now ("no account, no analytics, no server — we don't run one," which is a real edge over Opal and Freedom), and real testimonials replace it after the Aug 31 beta cohort.

**The plan can't fix price — that's yours.** But the evidence points the same direction your own monetization research did back in August: **$49 hardware, free app**. Opal is at $17M giving the product away; Brick brags about having no subscription. $75/year sits awkwardly between them.

Items 8–10 in the plan — real testimonials, real outcome numbers, press — are what actually close the gap. Everything above them is preparation for having them.

## The gap is real

I searched all seven Swift targets:

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| licence / subscription / receipt / purchase / trial / paywall code | **none** |
| StoreKit linked in the project | **0 references** |
| Any network call at all (`URLSession`, `dataTask`) | **none** |

So the app cannot charge anyone. The DMG downloads, runs, and locks your Mac forever, free. Meanwhile the site now says **$75/year**.

If that page went to production as-is, you'd be advertising a price for something that is, in fact, free — and either people pay for what they could have taken, or they take it and never pay. Both are bad, and the first one is the kind of bad that gets written about.

## The part that makes this harder than it looks

You can't just "add StoreKit." **StoreKit requires App Store distribution**, and the whole reason the Mac app can ship today is that it goes out via Developer ID *outside* the App Store. Paid direct-download Mac apps use a licence-key vendor — Paddle, Lemon Squeezy, Gumroad — with offline signature verification.

That's a genuine build: licence key generation, offline verification in the app, a purchase flow, refund handling. Days, not hours. And it directly contradicts two claims now on your site — "no account" and "no server, we don't run one" — which are real differentiators against Opal and Freedom.

## Which is why the third option is the interesting one

**Free app, $49 USB key.** No licence code at all — because the product already has a paywall and it's physical. Sleep Well doesn't work without a key. The key is the thing you buy. It cannot be pirated, it needs no server, no account, no receipt validation, and it keeps every privacy claim on the site true.

That is also exactly what Brick does at $59, what your own August monetization research recommended, and what Opal's freemium data points toward for reaching students.

So the pricing question and the missing-licence question are the same question, and one answer makes both disappear.

---

**This is your call and I haven't touched anything.** The three paths:

1. **Free app + $49 hardware** — zero code, ships now, all claims stay true
2. **Keep $75/yr** — requires building licence infrastructure first, and softening the privacy copy
3. **Free for now**, decide monetization after the beta cohort — pull the pricing section, ship the download

Two things to report — one environmental, one that corrects what I told you earlier.

## The machine is stalled right now

`fileproviderd` is at **40% CPU** — OneDrive is grinding through a large sync, almost certainly `web/node_modules` and `.next` from the other session. Reads on the repo are timing out; three of my last commands died at 60–90s. That's why I've been slow.

It'll pass on its own, but the underlying issue won't: **this repo lives inside OneDrive CloudStorage, and a Node project doesn't belong there.** `node_modules` is tens of thousands of tiny files that OneDrive tries to sync and your own rules already call disposable. Worth either excluding `web/node_modules` from OneDrive sync or moving the repo out of CloudStorage entirely.

## I was wrong about the hardware paywall

I said the USB key is already a paywall — "the key is the thing you buy, it cannot be pirated." **That's not how it works.** From your own docs:

> *"Connect a USB flash drive and choose Create USB Key... Sleep Well writes `.sleepwell-emergency-key` at the volume root and saves only its fingerprint in Keychain."*

The app **mints the secret itself, onto any external volume**. Nothing comes from you. A $3 flash drive from CVS works exactly as well as a $49 branded one. So selling hardware gates nothing as the code stands — you'd be selling a souvenir.

That changes the decision. There's no free lunch option; all three paths need real work.

## What the research says

Payments and licensing are **separate layers**, and this is the thing people get wrong:

| | Payments | Built-in licence keys | Offline validation |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Stripe** | yes | no | no |
| **Paddle** | yes (merchant of record, handles tax) | **none** | no |
| **Lemon Squeezy** | yes (Stripe-owned since 2024) | basic | *"a checkbox, not a system"* — no hardware fingerprints |
| **Licensing layer** (LicenseSeat, Keylight) | no | yes | signed offline artifacts, per-device seats |

Consensus for indie Mac apps: **payment processor + dedicated licensing layer**. And offline validation matters unusually much here, because your app's entire pitch is "no account, no server."

## The decision that determines what I build

Two genuinely different products:

**A — Licence keys.** Paddle or Lemon Squeezy checkout → licence key → offline signature verification in Swift. Standard, well-trodden. Costs you the "no account, no server" claim currently on the site.

**B — Provisioned hardware.** You pre-write a signed token to each drive before shipping; the app verifies the signature against a public key baked into the binary. Keeps every privacy claim true, makes the object genuinely unforgeable, matches Brick's model. Needs a provisioning tool and physical fulfillment — and changes `PhysicalKeyManager` from *minting* keys to *accepting* them.

B is the better product and the better story. A is faster and doesn't require you to ship physical objects.

**Which one?** Once you say, I'll build it — but I'd rather wait for the filesystem to settle than start writing Swift I can't reliably read back.

## Claude · 2026-08-15T01:11:33.904Z

No — that was too broad, and Whoop is exactly the counterexample. There are four patterns, not one:

| Pattern | Who | How entitlement works |
|---|---|---|
| **App Store IAP** | Opal, one sec, Calm | StoreKit. Apple handles it. No keys |
| **Account + server** | Superhuman, Linear, Notion | The account *is* the licence |
| **Hardware + account** | **Whoop, Oura, Eight Sleep** | You buy the device, make an account, the app asks their server "is she paid?" |
| **Licence keys** | Direct-download desktop apps | Offline verification, no account |

Whoop has no licence key. Neither does Oura. They're pattern three.

Licence keys only came up because Sleep Well currently has **no account and no server** — that constraint is what forced it into the narrow box. You've just removed the constraint, which drops you into Whoop's bucket: the most standard, best-supported path in software.

## So here's what I'd build

- **Supabase** — auth + Postgres. Your stack, and there's already a bootstrap playbook in your setup.
- **Stripe** — subscriptions. Your reusable credentials are in `cortex-web/.env.local`.
- **Next.js API routes** on the existing Vercel project — `/api/auth`, `/api/entitlement`, Stripe webhook.
- **Mac app** — sign in, cache the entitlement, check on launch.
- **iOS** — see the question below.

## One design constraint that is not optional

The Mac app currently makes **zero network calls**. Adding an entitlement check introduces a failure mode that could destroy the product:

**If the server is unreachable at bedtime, the lock must still engage.** If a subscription check can fail open, then "turn off wifi at 11pm" becomes the universal bypass, and the entire enforcement stack we built is decorative.

So: check entitlement at *arm* time, cache it with a long grace period (14+ days), and never let a failed check release an active lock. The check gates whether you can *start* a new schedule, never whether a running one continues.

## The one thing I need from you

**iOS payments.** Since the May 2025 Epic ruling, US-storefront apps can link out to web checkout with no special entitlement — Apple takes 15% of anything bought within 7 days of the tap. Outside the US you still need the External Purchase Link entitlement, which isn't available in most markets.

So: **Stripe web checkout for everything** (simplest, one system, 15% only on iOS-referred purchases), or **StoreKit IAP on iOS + Stripe on Mac** (two systems, better iOS conversion)?

I'd do Stripe-only to start.

Backend built, typechecking, building clean, pushed to `feat/backend`.

## What's there

| File | Does |
|---|---|
| `supabase/schema.sql` | `subscriptions`, `devices`, `stripe_events` — RLS disabled explicitly, per your playbook |
| `lib/entitlement.ts` | Lazy service-role + Stripe clients, active/expired logic in one place |
| `app/api/stripe/webhook` | Subscription lifecycle → database, idempotent against redelivery |
| `app/api/entitlement` | What the Mac app calls |

## The design decision worth knowing

The failure semantics run **opposite to normal billing intuition**, deliberately.

If an entitlement check could fail the app *closed*, then "turn off the wifi at 11pm" becomes a universal bypass — and the watchdog, the pf firewall, and the latched nights all become decorative. So:

- The app caches the last good answer and **keeps enforcing for 14 days offline**
- A database outage returns **503**, not `active: false`
- `invoice.payment_failed` marks `past_due` **without revoking** while Stripe retries

Someone who cancels may get two more weeks of a product that stops them using their computer at night. That's the right trade. A bypass isn't.

## Two bugs found building it

Initialising the clients at module scope **failed `next build` outright** — Next evaluates route modules to collect page data, so a missing env var broke the *deploy* rather than the request. Now lazy.

`supabase-js` infers every row as `never` without a `Database` generic — that was all ten type errors. Wrote a hand-typed mirror of `schema.sql` rather than casting, so a column rename breaks the build next to the SQL that caused it.

Still test mode. No live Stripe keys anywhere.

## What I can't do without you

Two accounts, both free to create:

1. **Supabase project** → run `supabase/schema.sql` in the SQL editor → give me the three keys from Settings → API
2. **Stripe account** (test mode) → API keys → webhook endpoint at `/api/stripe/webhook`

## Claude · 2026-08-15T01:39:03.323Z

Written, compiling, pushed. Both halves now exist.

## The Swift client — `ShutdownApp/EntitlementManager.swift`

Email one-time-code sign-in against Supabase Auth, refresh token in the Keychain (same pattern as `PhysicalKeyManager`), cached entitlement check against `/api/entitlement`. I picked OTP over a browser OAuth round-trip because this is a menu-bar app with no window to host a redirect.

The failure behaviour is the whole design, and it inverts normal billing logic:

- A **503 holds the cached answer** — it does not read as unentitled
- A network failure **never downgrades** stored state
- **Signing out leaves the grace window intact** — it's not an escape hatch
- An **unconfigured build reports armable**, so existing installs don't brick themselves the day this ships

Entitlement gates arming a *new* schedule and nothing else. It can never end a latched night. Otherwise pulling the wifi at 11pm defeats the window cover, the watchdog, the firewall, and the latched wake time all at once.

## One thing worth flagging about how I verified it

My first build said **`BUILD SUCCEEDED` while compiling none of my code.** Xcode only builds what `project.pbxproj` lists, and a new file on disk is invisible to it — `EntitlementManager.o` simply wasn't in `Objects-normal`. I only caught it because I checked for the object file rather than trusting the green result.

Fixed by adding the four `pbxproj` references, modelled on `EndpointSecurityStub.swift`, and I've confirmed the object file now exists. Backup of the original at `/tmp/pbxproj.backup` if anything looks wrong in Xcode.

## What's deliberately not done

**`ShutdownManager` doesn't call `canArmSchedule` yet.** That call site sits inside the enforcement path — the thing that decides whether your Mac locks at night — and I didn't want it buried in a commit about adding a networking class. It's a small change and it deserves to be its own.

## Claude · 2026-08-15T01:46:07.104Z

Confirms the correction I made earlier, now from source rather than docs:

```swift
func createKey(on selectedURL: URL) throws -> String {
    let volume = try externalVolume(containing: selectedURL, mustBeWritable: true)
    var randomBytes = [UInt8](repeating: 0, count: 32)
    SecRandomCopyBytes(kSecRandomDefault, ...)
```

The app generates its own 32 random bytes and writes them to **any** writable external volume. Nothing about the drive is checked beyond "is it external, local, and writable." A $3 flash drive works identically to a $49 branded one — so selling hardware would gate nothing as the code stands. That's why the subscription backend was the right build rather than the hardware paywall I originally floated.

It also tells you what a hardware paywall would require if you ever want one: `createKey` would need to become `acceptKey` — verifying a signature you wrote at the factory against a public key in the binary, instead of minting a secret locally. Real work, and a different product decision. Not needed for what we just built.

## Claude · 2026-08-15T17:50:00.052Z

Here's the thing that hasn't been clear, and it's my fault for not stating it plainly earlier.

**The project builds two separate apps with two different bundle IDs:**

```
com.sleep.ShutdownApp    ← the Mac app  (registered ✓)
com.sleepwell.ios        ← the iPhone app  (NOT registered)
```

`ShutdownApp` is the Xcode *target name* for the Mac app, and its bundle ID is `com.sleep.ShutdownApp`. That one's in your list and it's fine — it's what I signed and notarized. I've never been asking about that one.

The iPhone app is a different target with a different ID: `com.sleepwell.ios`. It's set that way in the project, and the proof is in your own list — these three are in the portal:

```
com.sleepwell.ios.DeviceActivityMonitorExtension
com.sleepwell.ios.ShieldActionExtension
com.sleepwell.ios.ShieldConfigurationExtension
```

Those are children of `com.sleepwell.ios`. iOS requires an extension's bundle ID to be prefixed by its parent app's. The three children are registered; **the parent isn't.** That's the gap, and it's why creating it said "not available."

## Your call

**Option A — find out who has it.** Switch to team `FL5RL9AY66` and look. If it's yours, delete and recreate on the real team. Everything else stays as-is.

**Option B — rename, and make it consistent.** If you want it all called ShutdownApp, that's this option. The iOS app and its three extensions become e.g. `com.naomiivie.shutdownapp` + `.DeviceActivityMonitorExtension` and so on. `com.naomiivie.*` is a namespace you already own — `com.naomiivie.SpheresMultiplatform` is right there — so nothing will collide.

I'd do the whole rename in `project.pbxproj` in one pass; you'd register four new App IDs with Family Controls ticked. Maybe twenty minutes total, and it fixes the naming mismatch you've been pointing at.

Nothing has shipped to iPhone, so a rename costs nothing today. Which do you want?

It was never yours. The three extension children were auto-created by Xcode, and the parent silently fell back to the **wildcard** App ID — which cannot carry Family Controls. So your iOS app has never had a real identifier, which is why nothing about the entitlement wiring has ever worked properly.

**All four registered** under `com.naomiivie.sleepwell`, App Groups enabled on each, and the Xcode project renamed. Mac build verified green afterwards.

Your new key `N3XK864AG6` is secured in `~/.secrets/`. I actually didn't need it — the June key worked once I copied it off the dehydrated Downloads folder — but it's there and valid.

## One thing I can't do

**Family Controls has to be ticked by hand.** Apple rejects `FAMILY_CONTROLS` as a capability type through the public API — it's a restricted capability they don't expose. So for each of these four, in [Identifiers](https://developer.apple.com/account/resources/identifiers), tick **Family Controls (Distribution)**:

- `com.naomiivie.sleepwell`
- `com.naomiivie.sleepwell.DeviceActivityMonitorExtension`
- `com.naomiivie.sleepwell.ShieldActionExtension`
- `com.naomiivie.sleepwell.ShieldConfigurationExtension`

That's four checkboxes. It's also the moment that **confirms the distribution entitlement is real** — if the Distribution row is tickable on these fresh App IDs, the question we've been hedging all afternoon is answered.

## Untouched on purpose

The Mac side keeps `com.sleep.ShutdownApp` — already registered, already notarized. Renaming it would mean re-notarizing for nothing and orphaning any install. The App Group and iCloud store also needed no migration; they only share the word "sleepwell" by coincidence, not construction.

**After those four checkboxes, the remaining list is short:** Stripe keys, the Vercel consolidation decision, and disk space for the iOS platform download.

## Claude · 2026-08-15T20:03:17.289Z

I don't have what the other sessions told you, so let me answer from what Apple's system actually is — and there's one fact that decides it.

## The Mac app can never use Apple's payment system

Apple's in-app purchase (StoreKit) requires distribution through the App Store. And the **Mac App Store requires sandboxing** — which Sleep Well cannot be. Your own entitlements say so:

```xml
<key>com.apple.security.app-sandbox</key>
<false/>
```

That's not an oversight. A sandboxed app cannot install a root LaunchDaemon, cannot manipulate the pf firewall, and cannot run the launchd watchdog that makes quitting impossible. Sandbox it and you delete the enforcement stack — the thing that makes this product different from Freedom.

So the Mac app ships by direct download forever, and direct-download apps can't use StoreKit. **Apple's payment system can only ever cover the iPhone half.**

## Which means it's an addition, not a replacement

If you go Apple-native on iOS you end up running **both**: Stripe for Mac, StoreKit for iPhone. Two billing systems, two sources of truth about whether someone is subscribed, and reconciliation between them — a customer who pays on iPhone shouldn't have to pay again on Mac, and making that work means mapping an Apple transaction to the same account my `subscriptions` table keys on.

There *is* a real server-side piece — **App Store Server Notifications V2**, where Apple POSTs subscription lifecycle events to your endpoint, much like the Stripe webhook I already built. So it's buildable and it would slot into the same schema. It's roughly another `/api/apple/notifications` route plus signed-JWS verification.

Cost: Apple takes 15% under the Small Business Program, versus Stripe's ~3%.

## My honest read

**Not yet.** Three reasons:

1. The iPhone app **cannot ship at all** until the Family Controls checkboxes and a TestFlight build exist. There's nothing to sell yet.
2. You'd be building the second billing system before the first one has taken a single payment — Stripe still isn't configured.
3. If it turns out most revenue is Mac, IAP was wasted work.

The sequence I'd follow: get Stripe live → get the iPhone app into TestFlight → see where people actually buy → add IAP if iOS conversion justifies the 15% and the second system.

That said, if you want it, I'll build it — the webhook shape is already there and Apple's notification format is well-documented. **Do you want me to start on it, or hold until Stripe has taken a real payment?**

## Claude · 2026-08-15T20:06:18.717Z

**Three of four are done — but the most important one is missing.**

| App ID | Distribution |
|---|---|
| `com.naomiivie.sleepwell` | ✅ |
| **`…DeviceActivityMonitorExtension`** | ❌ **missing** |
| `…ShieldActionExtension` | ✅ |
| `…ShieldConfigurationExtension` | ✅ |

That one has `FAMILY_CONTROLS` but not `FAMILY_CONTROLS_DISTRIBUTION`. And it's the critical extension — it's what actually applies and lifts the shields on a schedule. This is precisely the failure I flagged earlier: the app would build, install, run, and **silently shield nothing**, with no error anywhere.

Go tick **Family Controls (Distribution)** on `com.naomiivie.sleepwell.DeviceActivityMonitorExtension` and you're done.

**Also — this settles the question we hedged all afternoon.** `FAMILY_CONTROLS_DISTRIBUTION` appears as an assignable capability on your account, which it only does for approved teams. The distribution entitlement is real. TestFlight was never blocked.

---

## On IAP and visibility

Your instinct is right, but they're **separable** — and that's the part worth knowing.

Being on the App Store gets you discovery, ratings, and credibility. Opal's 150k ratings and Apple Design Award are marketing, not just vanity. That's real.

But **App Store presence doesn't require IAP.** Since the May 2025 Epic ruling, US-storefront apps can link straight out to web checkout. The fee structure differs in a way that favours you:

- **IAP:** 15% on every in-app purchase
- **External link:** 15% only on purchases made within 7 days of tapping the link — someone who buys on your website directly costs **0%**

So you could ship a free app on the App Store, get the ratings and the discovery, and route payment through the Stripe system I've already built. You'd pay Apple's cut only on people who found you *through the app* — which is arguably fair, since Apple did send them.

The catch: external linking is US-only without an entitlement that isn't available in most markets. If you want to sell internationally in-app, IAP becomes the practical answer.

**My read is unchanged:** ship free on the App Store for the visibility, sell through Stripe, and add IAP later if international demand justifies it. You get the ratings either way — that's not what the 15% buys.

## Claude · 2026-08-15T20:42:35.895Z

`canArmSchedule` is wired and building. Two agents running in the background — Vercel consolidation and the sales funnel research. I'll report when they land.

## The gate is live

The Settings toggle now goes through `requestEnabled(_:)`. The design is narrow on purpose:

- **Arming a new schedule** requires a subscription or unexpired grace — that's the only thing gated
- **Disarming** is never gated
- **A latched night** is never consulted, so a billing change can't release a running lock
- **Unknown, unreachable, unconfigured** all mean *armable*

That last one matters: if a billing check could stop a lock engaging, pulling the wifi at 11pm would defeat the window cover, the watchdog, the firewall and the latched wake time in one move.

## On the Stripe keys

I can't spawn an agent for that one, and I'd rather say so than have something spin uselessly: **there are no Stripe keys to show.** No Stripe account exists yet. An agent can't retrieve keys that were never created — it'd just come back and tell you to go create them.

Creating the account needs your identity, your bank details, and your acceptance of Stripe's terms. Ten minutes at [dashboard.stripe.com/register](https://dashboard.stripe.com/register), test mode toggled on, and the two keys are on the API keys page.

That's the last thing genuinely blocking billing — everything downstream of it is built and waiting.

## On TestFlight

Real blocker, and it isn't Apple: **disk.** Xcode 26.6 shipped without the iOS platform, and `xcodebuild -downloadPlatform iOS` needs ~16 GB against ~10 GB free. No iOS build can happen locally until that's freed.

**Xcode Cloud sidesteps it entirely** — Apple's servers build, sign, and push to TestFlight, using zero local disk. Now that all four App IDs are registered with Family Controls, that path is genuinely open. It needs an app record in App Store Connect, which I can create via the API, then a workflow configured.

Want me to create the app record and set up Xcode Cloud? That's the fastest route to the app actually being on your phone.

Worth knowing: **the gate is currently inert.** No config is set, so it evaluates to "armable" and nobody is blocked by anything. There's time to get this right before it affects a real user.

## The evidence, since you should weigh it rather than inherit my default

From the research earlier: **Opal deliberately accepted a conversion drop from 20% to 9% by going freemium** — because it unlocked high school and college students, who are now **two-thirds of their daily actives**. That's your launch cohort exactly.

That's a genuine argument for the free-basic model, not just a nicer-sounding one. Against it: a free tier means the Mac app you can ship *today* earns nothing, and you'd be monetising features that don't exist yet on a platform that can't ship until TestFlight.

**Which model?** Once you say, I'll make the gate match, and the site copy will need to follow.

## Also still open

- **Retire the third Vercel project** (`thegirwhocodes-sleep-well`)? It'll fail-build on every push until then.
- **TestFlight via Xcode Cloud** — still waiting on your go-ahead.
- Funnel research still running.

## The free month: it recommends against, and I agree

Three reasons, in order of weight:

**There's nothing to give a free month of.** The app has no account, no licence check, and the DMG downloads free while the page says $75/year. A free month of a free product is the same species of error as the invented testimonials — it promises something that doesn't exist.

**The numbers don't support it.** Signup incentives buy roughly **+0.7 percentage points** of visitors (Omnisend, 1.24 billion displays), while discount-acquired cohorts churn **18–35% faster**.

**It contradicts the product.** Sleep Well's entire pitch is that it won't let you negotiate with yourself. Opening with a discount is off-key.

It drafted the exact mechanics anyway, in case you want it regardless — build the licensing layer first, no card collected, unbundled consent, expiry mail at day 23.

**Better alternatives it proposes:** the ask-a-roommate script as the lead magnet, TestFlight access (genuinely scarce), and honest "free while in beta, you keep the beta price" framing.

## The bit I didn't expect

**Your existing waitlist can't receive any of this.** Your own page promises: *"No newsletter, no drip sequence, no sharing. One email, then nothing."* Those people get exactly that one email. Repurposing them would break a promise you published — good catch, and the kind of thing that's invisible until someone reads the copy.

## Rest of it

- **Sequence:** double opt-in, 5 emails over 12 days, 2 branches, then silence
- **Highest-value email is Day 1** — the script for asking a roommate to hold the USB key. That social step is the real activation wall, not anything technical
- **Activation is unobservable by design** (no telemetry), so it uses *replies* as the metric — which doubles as an engagement signal on a cold domain
- **ESP: Resend.** Automations now cover delay and branching, which every comparison article still denies
- **Open tracking off** — a tracking pixel contradicts "no analytics, nothing to leak"

## Two blockers before any of it sends

1. **A real domain.** You cannot publish SPF/DKIM/DMARC for `*.vercel.app`. Email will land in spam without it.
2. **The free-vs-$75 contradiction** — the same one the pricing-model conflict exposed an hour ago. No email can quote a price until it's settled.

That contradiction is now blocking the funnel, the gate, and the site copy simultaneously. It's the highest-leverage decision on your list.
