<result>Both research streams are in. Here's the full report.

---

# Evidence-based email design &amp; copy mechanics for a solo-founder welcome sequence

**Framing caveat that governs everything below:** since September 2021, Apple Mail Privacy Protection (MPP) pre-fetches tracking pixels whether or not a human opens the message. Litmus's current data (July 2026, &gt;1B opens) puts **Apple at 62.26% of all detected opens** and says **MPP affects roughly 55–60% of all opens** ([litmus.com/email-client-market-share](https://www.litmus.com/email-client-market-share)). For a sleep app — iPhone-skewed by definition — you are *more* contaminated than the benchmark average. Every "subject line X lifts opens Y%" study below that post-dates 2021 is measuring a partly-machine population, and every one that pre-dates 2021 is measuring a world with different clients. I flag which is which throughout.

---

## 1. Plain text vs. designed HTML

### 1.1 The primary tests

**HubSpot** — [blog.hubspot.com/marketing/plain-text-vs-html-emails-data](https://blog.hubspot.com/marketing/plain-text-vs-html-emails-data) (updated June 13, 2025; underlying tests are ~2014-era, drawn from "over half a billion" customer emails). Every A/B test they ran, the plainer variant won with statistical significance:

| Test | Result |
|---|---|
| Plain-text vs. HTML template with images | HTML had **21% lower CTR**; combined with opens, **51% fewer clicks** |
| Plain-text vs. GIF | GIF had 2.3% lower CTR; plain-text got **42% more clicks** overall |
| Simple HTML vs. HTML-heavy | Simpler had 5.3% higher CTR; **30% more clicks** overall |

**The nuance almost nobody repeats:** HubSpot's "plain text" emails were *HTML styled to look plain*, retaining hyperlinks and a tracking pixel — otherwise opens would have been unmeasurable. True `text/plain` cannot carry a pixel at all. So this is a test of *visual minimalism*, not of MIME type. That distinction is the single most useful thing in this section: **you are choosing an aesthetic, not a protocol.**

HubSpot's own caveat: "there may be exceptions. For example, ecommerce companies may have completely different results."

**Litmus** — [litmus.com/blog/the-results-are-in-a-b-testing-html-vs-plain-text-emails](https://www.litmus.com/blog/the-results-are-in-a-b-testing-html-vs-plain-text-emails) (Oct 23, 2020). Ran plain-text-style vs. HTML on webinar registration, split by audience:

- **Existing customers:** plain text won. 63% of converters received the plain-text/personal version.
- **Non-customers:** essentially no difference — both ~49% conversion.

Litmus's conclusion was explicitly *not* "plain text wins": "email engagement isn't always a good indicator of the success of an email campaign," and "these learnings are far from definitive."

**Counter-evidence worth taking seriously.** Userlist's roundup ([userlist.com/blog/saas-plain-text-emails](https://userlist.com/blog/saas-plain-text-emails/)) cites Carolyn Beaudoin (Boxcar Agency) running an **8-week A/B test that found plain text correlated with *higher* unsubscribe rates**, except for narrative-driven content where the two formats performed comparably. Databox's survey ([databox.com/html-vs-plain-text-emails](https://databox.com/html-vs-plain-text-emails), 2018, updated May 2024) collected flatly contradictory practitioner anecdotes — one respondent reported plain text performing "4X–9X better," another reported HTML winning by 6.52% on opens and 60.67% on clicks. Their split: **62% of marketers send a hybrid, 20% pure HTML, 16% pure plain text.**

### 1.2 The citation circle — worth seeing explicitly

Stripo's aggregation page ([stripo.email/blog/plain-text-vs-html-email-statistics-benchmarks-and-what-the-data-shows](https://stripo.email/blog/plain-text-vs-html-email-statistics-benchmarks-and-what-the-data-shows/)) lists a dozen plain-text stats and attributes them to SendCheckIt, Beehiiv, Mailtrap, and InfoTanks — **none with a year, none with a sample size**. Trace them and they collapse back into HubSpot's single 2014 test set and Litmus's single 2020 test. Stripo, to their credit, admits it: they note most statistics "come from SaaS companies and marketing tool providers" and that primary research with disclosed sample sizes is absent.

**So: the entire "plain text wins" literature rests on two studies, one 12 years old and one inconclusive.**

### 1.3 Does image-heavy HTML hurt deliverability? Largely a myth.

The named deliverability experts say no, and they say it bluntly.

**Laura Atkins, Word to the Wise** — [wordtothewise.com/2017/05/text-to-image-ratios-in-email](https://www.wordtothewise.com/2017/05/text-to-image-ratios-in-email/):

&gt; "If anyone tells you that your delivery problems are the result of a bad text to image ratio, run away."

Her model: the ratio is part of the *fingerprint* filters use to recognize a mail stream, not a cause. "Mail is being filtered because the recipients aren't interacting with it enough and the ratio is just one part of the way the ISP identifies it."

**Al Iverson, Spam Resource** — ["Do image-only emails harm deliverability?"](https://www.spamresource.com/2022/07/do-image-only-emails-harm-deliverability.html) (July 18, 2022). Short answer: **"no, not really."** His nuance is the real truth: *some* filters do score on image-to-text ratio — **SpamAssassin does** — but SpamAssassin handles self-hosted mail and small corporate gateways, not Gmail/Apple/Microsoft/Yahoo, which use engagement and reputation ML. His actual objections to image-only email are **accessibility**, not deliverability.

**The one real test.** Email on Acid tested against **23 popular spam filters** and found that at **500+ characters, image-to-text ratio does not affect deliverability at all** — every such email passed every filter regardless of image count. Below 500 characters, an image-free email got caught by 4 filters; adding one image dropped it to 3. Their recommendation is therefore *don't send emails under 500 characters*, not *limit your images*. (Source: [emailonacid.com/blog/article/email-deliverability/does-text-to-image-ratio-affect-deliverability](https://www.emailonacid.com/blog/article/email-deliverability/does-text-to-image-ratio-affect-deliverability/) — **note: this URL returned HTTP 500 on every fetch attempt during research; the findings are consistently reported across secondary sources but I could not read the primary article directly.**)

**Corroborating absence:** neither [Google's sender guidelines](https://support.google.com/a/answer/81126) nor [Yahoo's best practices](https://senders.yahooinc.com/best-practices/) mention image-to-text ratio anywhere. The specific ratios that circulate — 60/40, 80/20 — trace to no filter vendor and no published test.

### 1.4 Spam trigger words: also mostly dead

Litmus, ["Why spam trigger words are a thing of the past"](https://www.litmus.com/blog/why-spam-trigger-words-are-a-thing-of-the-past) (Jan 8, 2021): trigger-word lists are "mostly an email deliverability myth." What actually determines placement is IP/domain reputation, authentication (SPF/DKIM/DMARC), and subscriber engagement. They quote Gmail's anti-spam team: *"Think of how you can make the user love your emails."* Litmus ran no test of their own here — it's an argument from how filters work, not an experiment.

**Counterintuitive corroboration:** Mailchimp's own subject-line analysis of **~24 billion delivered emails across ~22,000 distinct words** ([mailchimp.com/resources/catchy-email-subject-lines](https://mailchimp.com/resources/catchy-email-subject-lines/)) found that **entirely capitalized subject lines produced *slightly higher* open rates**, and that "urgent" and "important" produced open rates "much higher than normal." That directly contradicts the conventional ALL-CAPS-is-spammy advice. (Caveats: the study is pre-MPP and open-rate-based, and criteria were US campaigns to 500+ recipients from senders with 10+ prior campaigns.)

### 1.5 The founder plain-text pattern

**Honest verdict: no rigorous evidence exists, in either direction.** What exists is (a) HubSpot's 2014 minimalism finding, (b) Litmus's audience-dependent 2020 finding, and (c) a large volume of founder anecdote. The pattern is *plausible* on mechanism — Gmail's Primary-tab classification favors low link density, short body text, and single sender identity — but the sourcing for that mechanism is entirely marketing blogs, not Google documentation.

**Does it stop working at scale?** I found no credible evidence for this claim, and I looked. The sources arguing it are low-quality and mutually contradictory (one blog asserts "high-quality HTML outperforms plain-text by 20.5% in inbox placement," with no method). The defensible statement is narrower: **format is not what filters key on, so a plain-text aesthetic neither protects you nor dooms you at scale — reputation, authentication, and complaint rate do.** What genuinely changes at scale is that a "hit reply and tell me" CTA becomes unanswerable, and an unanswered invitation to reply is worse than no invitation.

### 1.6 Always send multipart MIME

Litmus ([litmus.com/blog/best-practices-for-plain-text-emails-a-look-at-why-theyre-important](https://www.litmus.com/blog/best-practices-for-plain-text-emails-a-look-at-why-theyre-important), updated Aug 31, 2022): "HTML-only emails are a red flag for spam filters." A plain-text MIME part also serves smartwatches, voice assistants, low-bandwidth clients, and users who force text-only view. Litmus explicitly notes that plain-text-*style* HTML "still needs a real plain text version included." Any competent ESP does this automatically — verify yours does.

### 1.7 Dark mode, image blocking, and what MPP broke

**Dark mode** — Litmus's [Ultimate Guide to Dark Mode](https://www.litmus.com/blog/the-ultimate-guide-to-dark-mode-for-email-marketers) (Feb 27, 2025) sorts clients three ways:

| Behavior | Clients |
|---|---|
| No color changes to what you specified | Apple Mail, Gmail desktop webmail, AOL, Yahoo |
| **Partial invert** (light bg → dark, dark bg unchanged) | Outlook.com, Outlook mobile, Office 365 Mac |
| **Full invert** (everything flips, including your dark elements) | **Gmail iOS app**, Outlook 2021 Windows, Office 365 Windows |

**You cannot force light mode.** No CSS or header disables a client's dark transform. Litmus's dark-mode share was 28% of opens in 2021 → 35% in 2022; their Nov 2024 [design best practices](https://www.litmus.com/blog/email-design-best-practices) put it at "1/3+ of subscribers."

What breaks: transparent PNG logos with dark artwork (disappear entirely); hard-coded `#ffffff` backgrounds; JPEGs with baked-in white backgrounds (never inverted, so they glare); pale grey body text; button text and button background inverting independently into white-on-white. **The 90/10 fix is to design in midtones** — colors near the middle of the range survive inversion; the extremes get mangled — plus explicit `color` and `background-color` on every container and the two meta tags (`color-scheme`, `supported-color-schemes`). Skip the full `prefers-color-scheme` theme with `[data-ogsc]` duplicates; it only pays off for high-volume design-led brands.

**Image blocking — the percentage is structurally unknowable.** Litmus's [image blocking guide](https://www.litmus.com/blog/the-ultimate-guide-to-email-image-blocking) (updated Aug 2021) states it plainly: *"There's no real way to tell how much of your audience is impacted by image blocking, as the open rates are measured by having an image pixel load."* That is a perfect survivorship trap — anyone with images off never registers as an open, so no open-based dataset can ever measure them. **The famous "43% of Gmail users read email with images off" is a 2013 figure describing the moment before Gmail turned images on by default** ([litmus.com/blog/gmail-data-analysis-reveals-image-blocking-affects-43-of-emails](https://www.litmus.com/blog/gmail-data-analysis-reveals-image-blocking-affects-43-of-emails)) — obsolete since the month it was published.

Who still blocks by default: Outlook 2007–2019 Windows, Outlook for Mac, Office 365, AOL. Who displays: Gmail, Outlook.com, Yahoo, Apple Mail, all major mobile clients. New material change: **new Outlook for Windows** proxies images through Microsoft with *"Always use the Outlook service to load images"* **on by default for non-Microsoft mailboxes**, and it produces false positives that block legitimate images ([Office Watch, Jan 2, 2025](https://office-watch.com/2025/new-outlooks-blocked-images-problem/)).

**MPP and open rates.** Litmus's before/after natural experiment ([litmus.com/blog/email-client-market-share-september-2021](https://www.litmus.com/blog/email-client-market-share-september-2021)) is the cleanest measurement available: Apple's combined share was ~48.7% immediately pre-MPP and is now ~62% — a ~13-point gap, confounded by real device growth. Omeda's study of **80,000+ email marketing accounts** found open rates rose **~18 percentage points** (to over 40%) in the six months after launch (cited via [HubSpot](https://blog.hubspot.com/sales/average-email-open-rate-benchmark)). Validity's 2024 figure for Apple-dominant audiences: reported opens run **18–32 points above verified engagement**.

**One correction to a widely-mangled caveat:** MPP does **not** re-attribute other clients' opens to Apple Mail. Litmus is explicit — "this doesn't affect other email apps used on Apple devices like the Gmail app on an iPhone." A Gmail-app-on-iPhone open still counts as Gmail. The distortion is that a large share of Apple's *open events* correspond to no human reading anything.

**What to use instead of open rate:**
- **Click rate** — MPP pre-fetches images; it does not pre-click links. This is your primary metric.
- **Conversion / activation rate** — for you, first sleep session logged, or day-2 return.
- **Reply rate** — uncontaminated and, for a founder sequence, the whole point.
- **Unsubscribe and complaint rate** — uncontaminated.
- ⚠️ **CTOR is not a clean substitute.** MPP inflates its denominator, so CTOR is *deflated* post-2021. Directional only.
- Litmus's position is "the open rate is not dead yet" — usable as a *trend* indicator to catch deliverability cliffs, never as a target ([litmus.com/apple-mail-privacy-protection-resources](https://www.litmus.com/apple-mail-privacy-protection-resources)).

---

## 2. Subject lines

### 2.1 Length, and what actually displays

Where the display limits sit (aggregated from [emailtooltester.com](https://www.emailtooltester.com/en/blog/email-subject-lines-character-limit/) — page 403'd on direct fetch, figures from indexed content — and corroborated across sources):

| Client | Characters shown |
|---|---|
| Gmail app, iPhone | ~38 |
| Android mail apps | ~33–41 |
| Apple Mail, iPhone | ~48 |
| Apple Mail desktop | ~60–70 |
| Gmail web | ~70 |
| Outlook desktop | ~50–70 (screen-dependent) |

**The only length rule that survives scrutiny: front-load the meaning into the first ~33 characters.** Everything else is contested. Recommendations range from Mailchimp's "under 40 characters" to studies claiming 36–50 characters win. And notably, **two large studies found no correlation at all between subject length and open rate** — which, given that all of them measure a metric MPP has broken, is the more believable finding.

**Skeptical note:** every subject-line length study is an open-rate study. Post-2021 they are measuring machine fetches. Pre-2021 they measured a different inbox. There is no clean subject-line length data in existence right now.

### 2.2 Personalization

The "**personalized subject lines increase opens 50%**" stat traces to a real primary source: **Yes Lifecycle Marketing, published September 12, 2017**, analyzing **7 billion emails** ([Marketing Dive coverage](https://www.marketingdive.com/news/study-personalized-email-subject-lines-increase-open-rates-by-50/504714/)).

- Name personalization: **21.2% open**; other personalization: **22.0%**; no personalization: **14.1%**
- **Click-to-open rate 58% higher** with personalized subject lines — this is the more valuable number, since CTOR is at least partly behavioral
- Only **2.3% of emails used any subject-line personalization** at all in the studied quarter

Mailchimp's 24-billion-email analysis found **full name (first + last) has the largest positive impact**, with first-name-alone showing smaller benefits — and *negative* impact in the legal industry.

**Verdict: it works, it's pre-MPP so the open figure is clean, but it's 2017 and personalization has since become table stakes.** More recent claims of 10–14% lift from first-name tokening are open-rate-based and post-MPP; treat as directional. If you use it, use the CTOR finding as your justification, not the open finding.

### 2.3 Emoji: effectively null

The best available comparison is MailerLite's: subject lines **with** emoji **38.13%** open vs **without 37.86%** — a **0.27 percentage point** difference. That is noise. Omnisend and Mailchimp both recommend a maximum of 1–2 emoji and testing rendering across clients, but neither publishes a lift figure. **Emoji is a brand-voice decision, not a performance lever.**

### 2.4 ALL CAPS, punctuation, questions, numbers

This is where conventional advice and actual data diverge most.

- **ALL CAPS:** Mailchimp's 24B-email analysis found fully-capitalized subject lines produced *slightly higher* open rates. Every best-practice blog says the opposite. Mailchimp's is the only large-sample measurement I found. It is also pre-MPP and open-rate-based. **The honest read: ALL CAPS probably doesn't hurt opens and may marginally help — but it plausibly hurts brand trust and reply rate, which is what a founder sequence trades on.**
- **Exclamation marks:** contested. Some analyses find below-average opens; others find brands that use them *sparingly* see 10–20% lift. No large-sample primary source.
- **Question marks:** the most useful finding in this cluster — questions tend to raise opens but **most senders see ~8% lower click rates** on subject lines ending in a question mark. Since clicks are the metric MPP hasn't broken, this cuts against question-mark subject lines.
- **Punctuation volume:** Mailchimp recommends no more than 3 marks per subject line.
- ⚠️ The Smart Insights punctuation analysis ([smartinsights.com](https://www.smartinsights.com/email-marketing/email-creative-and-copywriting/punctuation-subject-line-help-hurt-email-performance/)) is the most-cited source here and **returned HTTP 403** — I could not verify its methodology.

### 2.5 Curiosity gaps

There is real peer-reviewed work here, which is rare in this field: **"When curiosity gaps backfire: effects of headline concreteness on information selection decisions,"** *Scientific Reports* (2024) — [nature.com/articles/s41598-024-81575-9](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-024-81575-9) (the page redirects through an auth flow; I could not fetch the full text). The finding as reported: there is such a thing as omitting too much — headlines that are **too vague *or* too concrete** both reduce engagement, implying an inverted-U. Specificity creates more curiosity than vagueness.

For a welcome sequence specifically, the trust cost dominates: a subject line that over-promises produces an open followed by a disengagement, and **disengagement is the signal filters actually weight** (per Atkins, §1.3). In a welcome flow you are establishing the sender relationship that determines every subsequent send's placement. Curiosity gaps are a bad trade here.

### 2.6 Preheader / preview text

Litmus's [Ultimate Guide to Email Preview Text](https://www.litmus.com/blog/the-ultimate-guide-to-preview-text-support) (Nov 8, 2024):

- **Recommendation: under 90 characters.**
- Subscriber settings vary enormously — anywhere from **5 lines (~278 characters) to 0 lines**.
- Rendering: on mobile, below the subject line; on desktop, to the right of it.
- Supported in all major clients now: Gmail ("Snippets"), Apple Mail ("preview"), Outlook ("Message Preview").

**The mistakes Litmus flags:**
1. "View this email in your browser" leaking into the preview because the intended snippet was too short — the single most common failure
2. Preview text that merely repeats the subject line, wasting a second hook
3. Overly long preview text truncated mid-message
4. All-image emails that produce no usable snippet
5. Preview text that contradicts the subject line

Front-load the essential point, same as with the subject line.

### 2.7 From name

The credible measurement is small: HubSpot found a person's name as sender produced a **0.53 percentage point** higher open rate and **0.23pp** higher CTR. The circulating "27% lift from personal sender names" has no traceable method. **Sender name is worth getting right for trust and reply rate — the open-rate case for it is weak.**

---

## 3. Email length and structure

### 3.1 Length — read the caveats before the numbers

**The Boomerang study (2016, 40 million emails)** is the most-cited length data and is **routinely misapplied**. HubSpot's own write-up ([blog.hubspot.com/sales/ideal-length-sales-email](https://blog.hubspot.com/sales/ideal-length-sales-email)) is explicit that it covers **1:1 sales emails** and measures **response rate**, not marketing email and not clicks.

- Optimal: **50–125 words**, peaking at **~51% response rate** in the 75–100 word band
- A 25-word email performs about as well as a 2,000-word one (~44%)
- Below 35% response above 2,500 words
- Emails with **no body text** performed worst in the entire dataset (11%)
- **Reading level matters more than length:** third-grade-level emails performed **36% better on opens** than college-level, and **17% better on response** than high-school level

**For actual marketing email, the better primary source is Constant Contact** — [press release, March 3, 2015](https://news.constantcontact.com/press-release-constant-contact-data-reveals-direct-correlation-between-email-campaign-effectiveness), analyzing **2.1 million customer emails to 100+ recipients over 13 weeks**:

- Highest CTR at **three or fewer images and ~20 lines of text**
- Retail showed an extremely narrow window: CTR **improved 50% from 17→19 lines, then dropped 50% from 19→21 lines**
- Substantial industry variation: nonprofits tolerated far more; real estate peaked around 35 lines; office supply at 15 lines

**Caveat: this is 2015 data, pre-MPP but also pre-mobile-dominance in its current form.** The "20 lines" figure is directionally useful, not a target.

### 3.2 How little of it gets read

**NN/g's eyetracking study** ([nngroup.com/articles/email-newsletters-inbox-congestion](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/email-newsletters-inbox-congestion/), 2006, 42 participants, 117 newsletters) remains the best behavioral data:

- Average **51 seconds** per newsletter after opening
- Only **19% fully read**; scanning dominates
- **35%** of the time users only skimmed a fragment or glanced
- **67% of users had zero fixations within newsletter introductions** — the intro paragraph is, empirically, unread

**Litmus read-time data, and why it's now suspect:**

| Year | Avg. seconds | Source |
|---|---|---|
| 2011 | 10.4 | [Litmus infographic, Mar 8, 2017](https://www.litmus.com/blog/email-attention-spans-increasing-infographic) |
| 2016 | 11.1 | same |
| 2018 | **13.4** | [Litmus press release, Jun 27, 2019](https://www.litmus.com/press/litmus-report-email-read-time-increased-by-21-percent) — 10B+ emails; read 61% / skimmed 23% / glanced 15% |
| 2022 | ~9 | reported via MarketingProfs (page 403'd) |
| 2024 | **8.97** | [Litmus, Nov 21, 2024](https://www.litmus.com/blog/email-design-best-practices) |

⚠️ **Read this trend skeptically.** Read time rose steadily through 2018, then appears to collapse ~33% — precisely across the MPP boundary. Machine pre-fetches plausibly register as near-zero-duration engagement, dragging the average down. The 2018 figure (13.4s) is the last clean one. The honest statement: **you have roughly 9–13 seconds, and the first screen carries the message.**

### 3.3 Single CTA vs. multiple — the primary source, and the zombie

**The "371% more clicks / 1617% more sales" claim does not survive tracing.** Here's the chain:

- Campaign Monitor's [investment post](https://www.campaignmonitor.com/blog/email-marketing/5-reasons-why-you-should-invest-in-email-with-stats/) states it and links to `wordstream.com/blog/ws/2015/06/10/digital-marketing-stats`
- Saleslion's [dedicated stat page](https://saleslion.io/sales-statistics/emails-with-a-single-call-to-action-increased-clicks-371/) attributes it to the same WordStream URL
- **I fetched that WordStream page. The statistic is not on it.** I also fetched WordStream's 2014 email statistics post — 404.
- Review My Emails' [CTA page](https://reviewmyemails.com/emailalmanac/content-and-creative/cta-call-to-action-design/how-many-ctas-should-email-have) asserts the single-CTA rule with **zero citations of any kind**

**Verdict: the 371%/1617% figure has no reachable primary source. It is a listicle citation pointing at a listicle that no longer contains it. Do not use it.**

**What does hold up: the Whirlpool test** — [MarketingSherpa case study, February 6, 2014](https://www.marketingsherpa.com/article/case-study/whirlpool-lift-clickthrough-testing-culture).

- **Control:** four CTAs (three "Learn more" buttons plus one "See Details")
- **Treatment:** the three "Learn more" buttons removed, leaving only "See Details"
- **Result: 42% higher clickthrough rate**
- **Limitations to state honestly:** no sample size disclosed, no statistical confidence reported, and **no conversion data at all** — Whirlpool's own marketer framed the goal as "quality of clicks," but the study never measured whether the extra clicks converted.

**The useful distinction the literature does draw:** *competing* CTAs split attention; *repeating the same* CTA does not. For a longer email, placing the same destination at the middle and end (with slightly varied button copy) gives skimmers an entry point wherever they land. One destination, multiple doors.

### 3.4 Buttons vs. text links

**Campaign Monitor** ([campaignmonitor.com/email/use-buttons-in-email-campaigns](https://www.campaignmonitor.com/email/use-buttons-in-email-campaigns/)): swapping a text link for a button produced a **28% increase in click-throughs**, with identical copy and offer, and they report replicating it "on a few different campaigns" with similar results. **Caveats: no sample size, no date, no significance testing, and they don't say how many replications.** A separate redesign yielded 127% more clicks, but buttons were only one of several changes.

**Counter-example they concede themselves:** SitePoint's newsletter gets excellent CTR from plain text links.

**The interaction with §1 matters here.** If you're writing a plain-text-style founder email, a designed button is tonally wrong — it re-signals "marketing." A bare URL or a linked sentence is the consistent choice, and Campaign Monitor's own counter-example says that's viable. **Use buttons when the email looks designed; use text links when it looks personal. Don't mix the signals.**

Litmus's [bulletproof buttons guide](https://www.litmus.com/blog/a-guide-to-bulletproof-buttons-in-email-design) (Apr 3, 2025) is unambiguous on one point if you do use buttons: *"Image buttons get lost when images are turned off... and they're not accessible."* Use padding-based HTML/CSS buttons, 42–72px tall (48–56px is the sweet spot), with explicit background color on both the `&lt;td&gt;` and the `&lt;a&gt;`.

---

## 4. Mobile rendering

### 4.1 "X% of email is opened on mobile" is no longer a measurable statistic

This deserves stating clearly because you will be tempted to cite it.

Litmus **no longer publishes** a mobile/desktop/webmail split. The only statement on their market-share page is qualitative: *"Mobile devices continue to be the primary reading environment."* The widely-quoted **41.6% mobile / 40.6% webmail / 16.2% desktop** is from Litmus's **2021** report — measured one month before the instrument broke. Campaign Monitor's client-share guide is worse: explicitly *"Updated December 2018."*

Why it's unrecoverable: (a) ~62% of opens are Apple, and MPP strips the user agent so Litmus cannot distinguish iPhone from iPad from Mac — they say so directly: *"MPP hides device data so we can't differentiate between iOS, iPadOS, or macOS"*; (b) 55–60% of all opens are machine fetches, not reading events; (c) the whole dataset is conditioned on images loading, so images-off users are invisible by construction.

**What you can defensibly say:** "Apple Mail and Gmail together account for ~90% of detected email opens" (Litmus, July 2026, &gt;1B opens). Cite the *client* split, not the *device* split — same source, current, and not destroyed by MPP.

**Current client share (Litmus, July 2026):** Apple **62.26%** · Gmail **27.03%** · Outlook **5.83%** · Yahoo **2.59%** · everything else &lt;2.4% combined.

⚠️ Treat any single month as ±10 points. Third-party citations of the same page show Apple at 49.29% (Jan 2025), 51.52% (Feb 2026), 64.66% (May 2026), 62.26% (Jul 2026). A 15-point swing in 18 months is measurement noise, not behavior change. **Never quote a decimal place.**

### 4.2 The constraints that actually matter

**Width: 600px.** The convention derives from Outlook's three-column reading pane — at 1366×768, navigation plus message list eat more than half the window, so anything past ~650px gets clipped. Use `width="100%"` with `max-width:600px`, content container at 560–580px inside padding.

**Font size: 16px body, never below 14px.** Litmus's [accessibility guide](https://www.litmus.com/blog/ultimate-guide-accessible-emails) specifies minimum 14px desktop / 16px mobile, line-height 1.5, left-aligned (centering paragraphs longer than two lines forces the reader to hunt for each line's start).

Two auto-scaling traps:
- **The Gmail app inflates fonts by over 50%** when any element exceeds the viewport width — documented by Justin Khoo at [FreshInbox](https://freshinbox.com/blog/gmail-on-ios-increases-font-size-on-some-emails/). Keep everything ≥14px and nothing wider than the container and it never fires.
- **iOS text-size-adjust.** Put `text-size-adjust:100%; -webkit-text-size-adjust:100%;` inline on `&lt;body&gt;` — **unprefixed first**, because in Safari iOS a trailing unprefixed declaration overrides the `-webkit-` one and defeats the fix ([MDN](https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/CSS/text-size-adjust)).

**Tap targets** — three standards, all primary-citable:

| Standard | Minimum | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Apple HIG | **44×44 pt** | [developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/buttons](https://developer.apple.com/design/human-interface-guidelines/buttons) |
| Google / Material 3 | **48×48 dp**, 8dp spacing | [support.google.com/accessibility/android/answer/7101858](https://support.google.com/accessibility/android/answer/7101858) |
| WCAG 2.2 AA (SC 2.5.8) | 24×24 CSS px | [w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/target-size-minimum](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG22/Understanding/target-size-minimum.html) |

Target **≥48px tall** buttons to satisfy both platform standards. `padding:16px 32px` on 16px text yields ~56px. Inline links inside a sentence are explicitly exempt from WCAG's target-size rule — but stacked footer links still need ≥8–10px vertical padding, and never put two links on one mobile line separated by a pipe.

**Single column — this is the highest-leverage decision.** Media query support is 80.48% of *tested clients* per [Can I Email](https://www.caniemail.com/features/css-at-media/), with these named failures:

- **Gmail with non-Google accounts (GANGA)** — the Gmail app when the user has added a Yahoo/Outlook/IMAP account. Gmail **strips embedded `&lt;style&gt;` blocks entirely**, so media queries, classes, and all non-inline CSS vanish. Your desktop layout is what renders.
- **Outlook Windows 2003–2019 and Outlook macOS 16.80** — nested media queries removed
- **Yahoo Mail** — limited query support; Yahoo Android needs a double-`&lt;head&gt;` hack

**A single ≤600px column with `width="100%" style="max-width:600px"` needs no media queries at all** and renders correctly everywhere. That one decision makes the entire compatibility question moot.

**Outlook's Word engine.** Rémi Parmentier's [definitive write-up](https://www.hteumeuleu.com/2020/outlook-rendering-engine/): Outlook 2007–2019 Windows render with **Microsoft Word**. No `background-image`, no `border-radius`, no flex/grid. The non-obvious killer: **`&lt;div&gt;` and `&lt;p&gt;` don't support `width`, `height`, or `padding`** — only tables and cells get the full property range. That's *why* email is still coded in tables. Padding on table rows collapses to the largest single value.

**But calibrate the panic:** Outlook is **5.83% of opens**, mostly B2B. New Outlook for Windows uses a Chromium engine with full modern CSS support; it becomes default in **April 2026**, though classic Outlook remains supported to at least 2029. For a consumer sleep app: don't build around it. Avoid `border-radius`, background images, and padding on divs, accept square button corners there, and skip VML entirely — the maintenance tax (duplicating every URL in both the VML and the HTML) isn't worth 6% of opens.

**Gmail's 102KB clipping limit.** Gmail truncates HTML past ~102KB and appends "[Message clipped]." Counts HTML and inline CSS, not externally-hosted image weight. **Target under 80KB** to leave room for ESP-injected markup. Clipping also hides your unsubscribe link, which is a compliance risk. ([litmus.com/blog/how-to-keep-gmail-from-clipping-your-emails](https://www.litmus.com/blog/how-to-keep-gmail-from-clipping-your-emails))

### 4.3 Accessibility — a 20-minute route into the top 0.1%

The [Email Markup Consortium Accessibility Report 2026](https://emailmarkup.org/en/reports/accessibility/2026/) analyzed **376,348 emails collected May 2025–May 2026**:

&gt; **99.88% contain "Serious" or "Critical" accessibility defects. Eight emails — 0.002% — had no issues.**

Top failures: missing `dir` (97.41%), missing `lang` (95.66%), **layout tables missing `role="presentation"` (83.78%)**, missing h1 (74.23%), links without discernible text (71.23%), **insufficient contrast (58.48%)**, **images missing alt text (47.88%)**, missing `&lt;title&gt;` (45.21%).

The top three are template attributes you set once. `&lt;html lang="en" dir="ltr"&gt;`, a `&lt;title&gt;`, `role="presentation"` on every layout table (without it a screen reader announces "table, row 1, column 1" before every line of your copy), alt text on every image, one `&lt;h1&gt;`, descriptive link text, 4.5:1 contrast for normal text / 3:1 for 24px+ text.

**Does it help deliverability?** Directly, essentially no — no filter scores `role="presentation"`, and neither Google's nor Yahoo's requirements mention accessibility. Indirectly, yes, through exactly Atkins's mechanism: an email people can't parse doesn't get engaged with, and engagement is what filters measure. The concrete win is that **live text instead of image-text simultaneously fixes screen readers, image blocking, dark-mode inversion, and Gmail's font inflation** — one discipline, four problems.

---

## 5. Benchmarks

### 5.1 The welcome-email zombie stats, traced

**"Welcome emails get 4x the opens and 5x the clicks" — REAL, and 16 years old.**

Primary source located, and it is not the 2014 report everyone assumes: **Experian CheetahMail, "The Welcome Email Report," released October 19, 2010** ([Experian plc press release](https://experianplc.com/media/latest-news/2010/welcome-emails-drive-transaction-rates-nine-times-higher-than-bulk-mailings) · [PR Newswire full text](https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/welcome-emails-drive-transaction-rates-nine-times-higher-than-bulk-mailings-105240663.html)).

Method: welcome emails vs. mass promotions **deployed by the same businesses** (good design — controls for list quality), **May 2009 – April 2010**. No sample size disclosed; the full report is no longer publicly hosted.

Findings vs. bulk: transaction rates **9× higher** (the actual headline), revenue per email **8×**, **opens 4×**, **clicks 5×**. Real-time triggered beat batch on every metric.

**Does it hold up?** *Direction yes, magnitude selectively.*

| Multiple | Experian 2010 | Klaviyo 2025 | Omnisend 2025 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Open | **4×** | 1.35× (51.26% vs 37.93%) | 1.17× (35.53% vs 30.41%) |
| Click | **5×** | 3.8× (4.92% vs 1.29%) | 5.3× (3.94% vs 0.74%) |

**The open multiple has collapsed; the click multiple has held almost exactly.** Two reasons the open multiple died: the 2010 baseline was ~15–17% (Experian's own Q3 2015 benchmark put average unique opens at 17.5%), and MPP has since inflated *both* sides toward 30–50%. **Cite the click multiple. Retire the open multiple. And say "Experian, 2010" out loud.**

**"Welcome emails get 50–60%+ opens" — MOSTLY BACK-DERIVED, but now coincidentally true.** The circulating "58%" does not appear in Experian's release, which reports only multiples. It looks like someone multiplied 4× by a contemporaneous ~14–15% average. Invesp's [heavily-scraped post](https://www.invespcro.com/blog/welcome-emails/) states a flat "50%" with **no source for any of its eleven statistics**. However, Klaviyo independently measured **51.26%** in 2025 — real, large-sample, and MPP-inflated. Omnisend measured **35.53%** on the same email type. **Honest range: 35–51% reported, with perhaps 20–30 points of that being machine opens.**

**"320% more revenue per welcome email" — UNTRACEABLE. Don't use it.** The chain: Campaign Monitor's [welcome infographic](https://www.campaignmonitor.com/resources/infographics/how-effective-are-welcome-emails/) → Invesp → nothing. Invesp cites no source; Easy-SMTP, the usual attribution point, **refused connection** when fetched. It also contradicts the only traceable primary source: Experian measured revenue per email at **8× (+700%)**, not +320%. Replace it with Klaviyo's **$2.35 RPR** or Omnisend's **$6.16 revenue per welcome email**.

**Evidence of active decay:** the same 2010 finding now circulates as 5× clicks, 10× clicks, and "196% lift in unique clicks." Campaign Monitor's infographic — an ESP, on its own product category — sources its stats to **thirteen third parties** including two competing ESPs and a magazine, with no dates. Treat that page as evidence of the problem, not as a source.

### 5.2 Current benchmarks, with vintage attached

**Klaviyo — the best available primary source.** [2025 Benchmark Report (AMER), PDF, Feb 2025](https://klaviyocms.wpengine.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/02/2025-Benchmark-Report_AMER.pdf), "billions of emails," excludes bot clicks, states its MPP caveat on p.7.

**Welcome flow specifically:**

| Metric | Average | Top 10% |
|---|---|---|
| Open rate | 51.26% | 74.19% |
| **Click rate** | **4.92%** | **15.69%** |
| Placed order rate | 1.97% | 9.89% |
| **Unsubscribe** | **0.96%** | 0.00% |
| Revenue/recipient | $2.35 | $20.92 |

Welcome is the second-highest-converting flow (abandoned cart leads at $3.07 RPR). **The top decile converts 5× the average off the same flow.**

Campaigns: 37.93% open / 1.29% click / 0.35% unsub / $0.10 RPR. All flows: 48.57% / 4.67% / $1.68.

⚠️ Two data-quality defects in Klaviyo's own PDF: the flow unsubscribe average is **labelled 9.81% but the plotted bar has zero length** — almost certainly a typo, don't repeat it; and the post-purchase chart reproduces the welcome flow's open bars while the text says 59.77%.

Klaviyo's [2026 update](https://www.klaviyo.com/products/email-marketing/benchmarks) (183,000+ customers) shows campaigns at 31% open / 1.69% click, flows at 5.58% click / 2.11% placed order, flows generating **~41% of email revenue from 5.3% of sends**, flow RPR **~18× campaigns**. Note campaign open rate *fell* from 37.93% → 31% — likely a measurement artifact, not behavior. **The 2026 page drops the welcome-flow breakout; use the 2025 PDF for that.**

**Omnisend** — [2025 calendar-year data](https://www.omnisend.com/blog/email-marketing-benchmarks/), 20B+ campaign emails, 27,000+ brands, 470M automated sends. MPP caveat stated.

Welcome email: **35.53% open / 3.94% CTR / 11.19% CTOR / 2.11% conv / $6.16 per email / 0.87% unsub.** Campaigns: 30.41% open / 0.74% CTR / 0.20% unsub. Automations = 2% of sends, 30% of revenue, **$2.87/send vs $0.18** (16×).

Relevant verticals: **Health 28.64% open / 0.79% CTR**; **Beauty &amp; Fitness 29.33% / 0.72%**; Sports 30.62% / 0.99%. **Health and Beauty &amp; Fitness both sit below the cross-industry average on both metrics.**

**GetResponse** — [updated 2024, analyzing 2023 data](https://www.getresponse.com/resources/reports/email-marketing-benchmarks), **4.4 billion messages**, senders with 500+ contacts. Overall: 39.64% open / 3.25% CTR / 8.62% CTOR / 2.33% bounce / 0.15% unsub / **spam complaints below 0.01%**. Health Care: 43.95% / 3.08% / 7.01% / **3.17% bounce** / 0.21% unsub.

**Welcome emails: 83.63% open / 16.60% CTR / 19.85% CTOR.** ⚠️ **This is the number every blog quotes and the one to trust least.** GetResponse's base skews creator/coach/course/newsletter — audiences who opt in for a lead magnet that the welcome email *contains*. It is also 2023 data at peak MPP inflation. GetResponse itself admits: *"growth in engagement rates can partially be attributed to Apple's devices distorting data with auto-opens."*

**Note the spread this creates: Omnisend 35.53%, Klaviyo 51.26%, GetResponse 83.63% — three primary ESPs measuring the same email type, disagreeing by 2.4×.** That spread is the strongest single argument in this report against treating any welcome open-rate benchmark as a target.

**Mailchimp — much older than people think.** The [live page](https://mailchimp.com/resources/email-marketing-benchmarks/) states *"last updated in December 2023"* and now contains **only five industry rows** — Business+Finance, Non-Profits, Education, Ecommerce, All Users (35.63% open / 2.62% click / 0.22% unsub). **No health, fitness, mobile, or SaaS row exists anymore.** Every blog citing "Mailchimp says Health and Fitness gets 21.48%" is quoting a deleted table.

The archived version (Wayback, snapshot ~June 2023) shows that table and says its own data was *"last updated in October 2019"* — which makes it **the cleanest pre-MPP open-rate data available**:

| Industry (Oct 2019) | Open | Click | Unsub |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Health and Fitness** | 21.48% | 2.69% | **0.40%** |
| **Mobile** | 19.29% | 2.27% | 0.37% |
| **Software and Web App** | 21.29% | 2.45% | 0.37% |
| Average totals | 21.33% | 2.62% | 0.26% |

**The signal for you: Health &amp; Fitness had the joint-highest unsubscribe rate in the entire table, and Mobile and Software both ran well above average.** Your three nearest verticals are the churniest ones Mailchimp measured. Assume churn pressure, not open pressure, is your constraint.

**Campaign Monitor** (2022 report, 2021 data, 100B emails, MPP caveat stated): overall 21.5% open / 2.3% CTR / 10.5% CTOR / 0.1% unsub. **Wellness &amp; Fitness: 19.2% open / 1.2% CTR / 6.0% CTOR / 0.4% unsub.** No current (2024–2026) Marigold/Campaign Monitor primary benchmark report exists.

**HubSpot** is not primary data here — their [benchmark article](https://blog.hubspot.com/sales/average-email-open-rate-benchmark) reports 42.35% open / 5.3% CTOR for 2025 but the underlying data is **MailerLite's**. Useful from them: health thresholds of **unsubscribe below 0.5%/campaign, bounce under 2%**.

### 5.3 Welcome series vs. single email

**This is the weakest-evidenced area in the entire literature. No ESP publishes per-email drop-off across a welcome sequence.** Klaviyo measures the welcome flow as a unit with no per-message breakdown.

The circulating "a 3-email welcome series generates ~90% more orders" and "51% higher revenue vs a single message" are attributed to Omnisend — **but I could not find either figure on Omnisend's own [welcome series page](https://www.omnisend.com/blog/welcome-series/)**, which instead offers a 3.7% conversion rate sourced to a "2023-H1 Stats report" plus two implausible claims (998% greater click rate, 3,274% revenue lift) with no method. Treat these as vendor marketing.

Practitioner consensus (not benchmark data): **3–5 emails over 7–14 days**, email 1 immediate, email 2 at day 2–3, email 3 at day 5–6. Klaviyo's own published cadence guidance covers browse abandonment, not welcome. Klaviyo does recommend **suppressing welcome-series subscribers from other calendar sends** until the sequence completes ([klaviyo.com/blog/welcome-email-examples](https://www.klaviyo.com/blog/welcome-email-examples)).

Expected decay shape, from agency data (not ESP-primary): email 1 at 48–62% open, emails 2–3 at 30–42%, series click 4–7%. Useful as shape, not target.

**Run 3–5 emails and instrument your own drop-off. Anyone handing you a published per-email benchmark is guessing.**

### 5.4 The hard floor: Gmail and Yahoo, effective February 2024

**[Google's sender guidelines](https://support.google.com/a/answer/81126)** — bulk sender = ~5,000+ messages to personal Gmail accounts in 24 hours, and **once classified, the status is permanent**; reducing volume doesn't remove it.

- **Spam rate: keep below 0.10%, never reach 0.30%.** Both numbers are in the guidance — 0.30% is the cliff, **0.10% is the operating target** you should build to, because complaint rates spike on individual sends.
- SPF **and** DKIM, aligned; DMARC published (`p=none` suffices); From: domain aligned with SPF or DKIM
- Valid forward/reverse DNS (PTR), TLS, RFC 5322 compliance, valid Message-ID
- **One-click unsubscribe per RFC 8058** — `List-Unsubscribe-Post: List-Unsubscribe=One-Click` plus `List-Unsubscribe: &lt;https://...&gt;` — **and** a visible unsubscribe link in the body
- Unsubscribes: Google *recommends* fulfilling within 48 hours
- From June 2024, senders above 0.3% became ineligible for mitigation support until below 0.3% for 7 consecutive days; from November 2025, Gmail ramped enforcement with temporary and permanent rejections

**[Yahoo's best practices](https://senders.yahooinc.com/best-practices/)** — spam rate **below 0.3%**; SPF and DKIM both required; **DMARC at minimum `p=none` and DMARC must pass**; RFC 8058 POST method "highly recommended"; **"honor unsubscribes within 2 days"** — stated as a *requirement*, not a recommendation.

**Build to the stricter of the two: complaints &lt;0.10%, unsubscribes processed within 48 hours, full SPF+DKIM+DMARC, RFC 8058 one-click.**

### 5.5 Unsubscribe: normal vs. alarming

| Context | Rate | Source |
|---|---|---|
| GetResponse overall (2023) | 0.15% | GetResponse |
| Omnisend campaigns (2025) | 0.20% | Omnisend |
| Mailchimp all-users (Dec 2023) | 0.22% | Mailchimp |
| Klaviyo campaigns (2025) | 0.35% avg / 0.11% top-decile | Klaviyo PDF |
| Mailchimp Health &amp; Fitness (Oct 2019) | 0.40% | Wayback archive |
| **Klaviyo welcome flow (2025)** | **0.96%** | Klaviyo PDF |
| **Omnisend welcome email (2025)** | **0.87%** | Omnisend |

**Bands for a consumer app:**
- Regular lifecycle email: &lt;0.2% good · 0.2–0.5% normal · **&gt;0.5% investigate** · &gt;1% is a consent or acquisition problem
- **Welcome email: ~0.9% is the measured average and is NOT alarming.** Klaviyo (0.96%) and Omnisend (0.87%) independently landing near 1% is the strongest corroboration of any figure in this report. The welcome email is where mis-targeted signups self-select out. **Better an unsubscribe at email 1 than a spam complaint at email 6.**
- Spam complaints: GetResponse reports overall rates **below 0.01%** — two orders of magnitude under Gmail's cliff. If you're near 0.10%, you have an acquisition problem, not a deliverability problem.

---

## What I'd actually do with this

**Design:**
1. **Single column, 600px max-width, no media queries.** Immune to GANGA, Outlook, and Yahoo's gaps in one decision.
2. **16px body, 1.5 line-height, left-aligned.** Stops Gmail's font inflation and meets accessibility minimums simultaneously.
3. **Mostly live text.** A welcome email should be anyway — it solves image blocking, dark mode, alt text, and image-ratio anxiety at once.
4. **Midtone palette**, explicit `color` and `background-color` everywhere, no pure `#000`/`#fff`, logo with a light stroke or on a mid-tone plate, plus the two `color-scheme` meta tags. Skip the full dark theme.
5. **`&lt;html lang="en" dir="ltr"&gt;`, `&lt;title&gt;`, `role="presentation"` on every table, alt on every image, one `&lt;h1&gt;`, descriptive link text, 4.5:1 contrast.** ~20 minutes, puts you past 99.88% of senders.
6. **Under 80KB HTML** so Gmail doesn't clip your unsubscribe link.
7. **Pick a lane on tone.** Plain-text style → text links, no buttons, no logo. Designed → one padding-based HTML button ≥48px tall. Don't mix.

**Copy:**
8. **Front-load the first 33 characters** of the subject line. Skip emoji (null effect), skip question marks (~8% click penalty), skip curiosity gaps (trust cost dominates in a welcome flow).
9. **Preheader under 90 characters**, complementing rather than repeating the subject, and verify "View in browser" isn't leaking into it.
10. **~20 lines / 50–200 words**, third-grade reading level, one destination repeated as needed. **Cut the intro paragraph** — NN/g found 67% of users never fixate on it.
11. **One CTA destination.** Whirlpool's 42% is the only real number here, it's from 2014, and it measured clicks not conversions — so treat single-CTA as a sound default, not a law.

**Measurement:**
12. **Report open rate, target click rate.** Expect 35–55% reported opens on welcome; discount 20–30 points for machine opens given an iPhone-skewed sleep audience.
13. **Targets:** welcome click ≥5% (Klaviyo 4.92%, Omnisend 3.94%) · welcome unsub ≤1.0% · complaints &lt;0.10% · bounce &lt;2% (health lists run hot — GetResponse's health vertical hits 3.17%, so validate hard at signup) · campaign unsub &lt;0.35%.
14. **Substitute your own activation event** for "placed order" — first sleep session logged, day-2 return. The transferable insight from Klaviyo isn't the number, it's the variance: **the top decile converts 5× the average off the same flow.** What you control dwarfs any benchmark you'd copy.

**Three things I'd tell you not to repeat in public:** the 371%/1617% single-CTA stat (untraceable), the 320% welcome revenue stat (untraceable, and contradicted by its own supposed ancestor), and "welcome emails get 4x the opens" (real, but from 2010, and the open multiple has since collapsed to ~1.2–1.4×).

**Research limitations, stated honestly:** `emailonacid.com` returned HTTP 500 and `emailtooltester.com`, `smartinsights.com`, `constantcontact.com/blog`, and `marketingprofs.com` returned 403 on direct fetch — claims from those sources are secondhand and flagged where used. The *Scientific Reports* curiosity-gap paper redirects through an auth flow and I could only read its abstract-level findings. The WebSearch budget was exhausted before I could verify welcome-email send-timing data (immediate vs. delayed) from a primary source.</result>

## Claude · 2026-08-15T20:59:36.514Z

Second research pass landed — [EMAIL_MECHANICS.md](EMAIL_MECHANICS.md), source-traced rather than blog-aggregated. Two findings actually change the plan.

## Open rate is unusable

Apple Mail pre-fetches tracking pixels whether or not a human reads anything. Apple is **62% of detected opens**, and **55–60% of all opens are machines**. An iPhone-skewed sleep app is worse than that average.

So: report opens to catch deliverability cliffs, never target them. **Clicks, replies, unsubscribes and complaints** are the real metrics.

Three ESPs measuring the *same* email type disagree by 2.4× on welcome open rate — 35%, 51%, 84%. That spread is the argument against copying anyone's benchmark.

## Your verticals are the churniest measured

From Mailchimp's last clean pre-MPP data:

| Industry | Unsubscribe |
|---|---|
| **Health and Fitness** | **0.40%** — joint highest in the table |
| Mobile | 0.37% |
| Software | 0.37% |
| All industries | 0.26% |

**Churn pressure, not open pressure, is your constraint.** The sequence should earn the next send rather than squeeze the current one — which happens to be what the funnel plan already does.

## Three stats not to repeat

The famous "single CTA = 371% more clicks" traces to a page that **doesn't contain it**. "320% more welcome revenue" is untraceable and contradicts its own supposed source. "Welcome emails get 4× opens" is real but from **2010** — that multiple has since collapsed to ~1.2×. The *click* multiple held at ~5×; cite that.

## Highest-leverage design decision

**Single column, 600px, no media queries.** Gmail strips embedded `<style>` blocks for non-Google accounts, silently deleting every media query — a single column needs none and renders correctly everywhere.

Also: 99.88% of 376,000 emails studied have serious accessibility defects. Eight had none. Twenty minutes of template attributes puts you past effectively everyone.

---

All research is in and pushed. Short version:

## Email stack — decided

**Loops** (free, 1,000 contacts) for the mailing list. It has a `Contact added` trigger, so a form submit starts the sequence with no code, and double opt-in is a toggle. **Resend** for transactional and licence-key delivery, on a separate subdomain. **$0/month for both.**

Kit is out — its free plan literally can't run sequences.

## Three things that change what we'd have done

**DMARC changed in May 2026.** `pct=` was removed from the spec. Every tutorial online still tells you to use it.

**Complaint-rate thresholds are meaningless at your size.** Below ~333 Gmail deliveries, *one* annoyed person puts you over Google's 0.3% cliff — that's integer arithmetic, not a list-quality problem. Track absolute complaint count instead. Same logic kills IP warming: Resend's ramp *starts* at 150 sends/day, which already exceeds your entire list.

**The unsubscribe URL must only act on POST.** Security scanners crawl URLs in email — a GET-able endpoint silently unsubscribes real people, and you don't find out until the list shrinks for no reason.

## Where the whole project stands

**Done today:** notarized Mac DMG · Supabase live · subscription backend · entitlement gate rebuilt on the four-switch tier model · four App IDs registered with Family Controls · bundle IDs renamed · Vercel consolidated and git-connected · four stale docs corrected · four research documents.

**Blocked on you, and it's a short list now:**

1. **A real domain** — blocks the entire funnel. SPF/DKIM/DMARC can't exist on `*.vercel.app`. This is now the single biggest blocker.
2. **Stripe keys** — the backend is built and waiting
3. **Disk** — ~10 GB free; TestFlight needs ~16 GB for the iOS platform, or Xcode Cloud to sidestep it
4. **The third Vercel project** — retire it or leave it failing on every push

The domain is the one I'd do first. Everything about the mailing list depends on it, and it's ~$14.

Checked the hole you flagged. iOS doesn't have it, and the reason is a design difference you might want to adopt rather than a lucky escape.

I don't clamp on write paths at all. The clamp lives in `effectiveSchedules`, a computed property, and `startBedtimeSchedule()` is the only thing that arms — it iterates `effectiveSchedules`, never `allSchedules`. So every route in re-clamps automatically:

  isEnabled didSet          -> startBedtimeSchedule() -> effectiveSchedules
  SettingsView time change  -> updateSchedule()       -> startBedtimeSchedule() -> effectiveSchedules
  adoptFromCloud            -> updateSchedule()       -> same

Set 10pm while disarmed then enable, and it arms at midnight. Verified SettingsView:108-112 sets the scalars then calls updateSchedule(), so the armed-then-edited path is covered too.

The point: clamping at the enforcement boundary rather than at each mutation site means a *future* write path cannot bypass it. Your two-site clamp is correct today, but it is correct by enumeration — the third write path someone adds in six months is the bug. If macOS has a single arm function, moving the clamp inside it and deleting the setter-side clamp would make the hole structurally impossible instead of currently-absent. Your call, you know that code.

Same reasoning is why `isCurrentlyInBlockingHours` and `activeSchedule` read the clamped set — otherwise the lock indicator claims the phone is blocked at 10pm for a free user whose window actually starts at midnight. Worth checking the Mac has no equivalent display path reading raw preferences.

Good catch on your own devices table, and thank you for saying it plainly — keying on display name was the more dangerous of the two failure modes, since undercounting seats is invisible until someone notices they are getting more than they paid for. `x-sleepwell-device-id` with unique (user_id, device_id) and writing no row when the header is absent is exactly right.

I have NOT built the Keychain device ID on iOS yet, because there is no entitlement client there to send it — iOS reads `entitlementActive` from the App Group and nothing more. When you're ready to extend the client to iOS, tell me and I'll add the Keychain UUID (service com.sleepwell.device, kSecAttrAccessibleAfterFirstUnlock, no iCloud sync) and wire the header. Happy for you to own the client and me to own the tier derivation, or to take the whole thing — either split works, just not both of us writing it.

Keeping `entitlementActive` semantics as-is: a plain bool meaning "this install currently has a paid entitlement", written by you, read by me, no expiry logic on my side. If you need to express grace periods or plan level to iOS, add keys alongside it rather than overloading it, and I'll read them.

Agreed on not building families speculatively. Also agreed your day-8 argument is the stronger form of the free-device-limit case — asking for an account from someone explicitly promised they wouldn't need one is worse than asking on day 0. I'll pass that to Naomi; it may change her mind where my version didn't.

## Claude · 2026-08-15T21:06:22.649Z

The other session's review caught a live bug, and I've fixed it.

## What was wrong

My free-tier clamp was applied wherever the bedtime *gets written*. That works for the two write paths that exist — and breaks silently the moment anyone adds a third. The iOS session clamps at the point of *use* instead, which makes the bypass structurally impossible rather than just currently absent.

Adopting that pattern immediately exposed a real bug on the Mac: **Settings displayed the time you chose, not the time actually enforced.** A free user who set 10pm would see "10:00 PM" while the lock really engaged at midnight — believing they were protected for two hours when they weren't. For this app specifically that's about the worst class of bug there is.

Now enforcement, display and scheduling all read the same clamped value, and the UI can say when the free tier is overriding your choice rather than quietly disagreeing with you.

That's the second thing that session's review has caught in my code today — the device-keying bug was the first. The cross-checking is doing real work.

## Where you are

**Built and green:** notarized Mac app · Supabase live · subscription backend · tier gate on the four-switch model · four App IDs with Family Controls · bundle IDs renamed · Vercel consolidated · five research documents · four stale docs corrected.

**Waiting on you:**

1. **A domain** (~$14) — blocks the entire mailing list; SPF/DKIM/DMARC can't exist on `*.vercel.app`
2. **Stripe keys** — backend built and idle
3. **Disk** — ~10 GB free, TestFlight needs ~16 GB or Xcode Cloud
4. **The third Vercel project** — retire it, or it fails on every push

## TL;DR per section

**1. The Problem** — 34.8% of US adults sleep under 7 hours; 77% of high schoolers under 8. Cause isn't insomnia, it's *bedtime procrastination*. The killer finding: people restricted to 6 hours for two weeks accrued impairment equal to two sleepless nights **while their subjective sleepiness plateaued** — so the person deciding at 1am is experimentally incapable of judging their own impairment. Of 367 self-control tools surveyed, **14% add any friction to removing the block; ~2.5% resist uninstall.**

**3. The Evidence** — every within-study comparison of binding vs non-binding commitment favours binding, usually decisively. Laibson's *Golden Eggs* is effectively a theory of your product: illiquidity alone, with no penalty, is a sufficient commitment technology. Tempered by three things: **hard-commitment take-up never exceeds ~28% in any domain**, the welfare case is contested, and **no RCT of a physical-key lock exists.** Your mechanism is unevaluated.

**4. Competition** — the single most useful table you have. **Nobody ships a lock you cannot undo.** Brick has 5 emergency unbricks. Roots' support refills them. Forest has a "Give Up" button. Focus hands you an unlock token instantly. Only Cold Turkey Frozen and one sec are genuinely hard — and one sec needs a second person. Your intersection is **verified empty**.

**5. Economics** — says $75 Mac is your **weakest SKU** and $100 all-devices should be the headline. Also flags a lifetime gap. Note this predates the tiers you just set.

**6. Who Pays** — ranked by *demonstrated spend*, not market size. Recovery/behavioural addiction is #1 (13.4M, already paying $99–198/yr). ADHD #2 (15.5M diagnosed). **Students rank 7th of 7 — 74% cap at $10/month**, which is directly relevant to the $29/4yr tier that just landed.

**7. Platform & Legal** — this is the one the other session has been citing for the auto-renewal exposure.

**Hard Questions** — 44 KB of the questions an investor will actually ask, and it doesn't flinch: **7 are marked ⚠️ UNRESOLVED**. Q1 is Cold Turkey at $45 once vs you at $375 over five years — **8.3×**, stated without softening.

It wants the renewal price shown at equal visual weight at purchase, a reminder before the first full charge, and one-click cancellation. Cheap now, expensive after chargebacks.

Here's the whole go-to-market section, document by document.

## LANDING_PAGE_PLAN.md

Benchmarked your page against **Opal ($17.1M ARR), Brick, one sec, Eight Sleep** — their live pages and reported numbers, not agency listicles.

Two findings drive it: **Opal deliberately took a conversion drop from 20% to 9%** by going freemium, because it unlocked students, now two-thirds of their daily actives. And **Brick sells $59 once while explicitly marketing "no subscriptions."**

Ranked gaps: no social proof at all, no visual of the USB key (Brick's whole growth loop is that the object photographs well), no risk reversal on the highest-anxiety purchase in the category, a hero that leads with mechanism instead of stakes, and engineer-facing copy.

## SALES_FUNNEL_PLAN.md — 1,108 lines, 11 sections

**§0 Constraints** — three things that shape everything: no users, no licence layer, and an existing waitlist that was promised no drip.

**§1 Benchmarks** — what Opal, Brick, Duolingo, Headspace actually send. Modal pattern is 4–8 emails over 7–14 days.

**§2 Lead magnets** — what converts an anonymous visitor, and why most incentives underperform.

**§3 Email UI and copy** — plain vs designed, subject lines, single-CTA.

**§4 The first 72 hours** — activation, not nurture. The window that decides whether anyone ever uses the thing.

**§5 The sequence** — the deliverable. Confirmation + 5 emails over 12 days + 2 branches, **then the list goes quiet by default.** Every subject line under 40 chars, copy drafted verbatim, `[PROOF SLOT]` markers where real testimonials go. From a real monitored address, every email ending with a reason to reply. The confirmation email carries the download link, so confirming is progress rather than a toll.

**§6 The free month — no, not as proposed.** Nothing to give (the DMG is free), the economics are thin (+0.7pp of visitors, while discount cohorts churn 18–35% faster), it fights the positioning, and bundling marketing consent with the offer is the shape ICO guidance warns about.

**§7 Migrating the waitlist** — you can't. They were promised "one email, then nothing."

**§8 Deliverability** — the non-optional list.

**§9 ESP** — Resend vs Loops.

**§10 What NOT to do** — the sharpest page in the document. Seven rules, each with a reason.

**§11 Build order.**

## EMAIL_MECHANICS.md

**Open rate is unusable** — Apple is 62% of detected opens and 55–60% are machines. Measure clicks, replies, unsubscribes.

**Your verticals are the churniest measured** — Health & Fitness had the joint-highest unsubscribe rate in Mailchimp's last clean data. Churn, not opens, is your constraint.

**Three famous stats don't survive tracing:** the 371% single-CTA figure points at a page that doesn't contain it; "320% welcome revenue" is untraceable; "welcome emails get 4× opens" is real but from 2010 and has collapsed to ~1.2×.

Highest-leverage design call: single column, 600px, no media queries.

## EMAIL_DELIVERABILITY.md

**DMARC changed in May 2026** — `pct=` was removed. Every tutorial online is stale.

**Complaint thresholds are meaningless at your size** — below ~333 Gmail deliveries, one annoyed person puts you over Google's cliff. Track absolute count. Same maths retires IP warming: Resend's ramp starts at 150/day, more than your whole list.

**The unsubscribe URL must only act on POST** — scanners crawl email URLs and will silently unsubscribe real people.

**Recommendation:** Loops free for marketing, Resend for transactional, separate subdomains, $0/month.

---

Two things recur across all four: **nothing may imply users you don't have**, and **nothing can send until you own a real domain.**

[FUNNEL_BUILD_PLAN.md](Evidence/8.%20Go-to-Market/FUNNEL_BUILD_PLAN.md) — pushed, indexed in the dossier.

## The shape of it

**Phase 0 — two blockers, neither technical**
- **Buy a domain (~$14).** Not an optimisation — without it SPF/DKIM/DMARC can't exist and Gmail/Yahoo won't accept bulk mail at all. `sleepwell.app` is broker-parked; `getsleepwell.app` or `sleepwellapp.com` cost normal money.
- **Resolve the price contradiction.** Email 5 quotes a price while the site hands over a free DMG. Blocks that one email only.

**Phase 1 — Infrastructure** (me, ~30 min once the domain exists). Six DNS records, then DMARC walked `none → quarantine → reject` over four weeks with `rua=` pointed at Postmark's free monitor. Loops + Resend + Postmaster Tools. **$0/month.**

**Phase 2 — Capture** (me, ~5 hours). New form with typo correction, role-address blocking, Turnstile, unchecked consent. Double opt-in. A POST-only unsubscribe endpoint.

**Phase 3 — The sequence** — transcription, not writing. The copy already exists verbatim.

**Phase 4 — Measurement.** Clicks and replies. Complaints as an absolute count.

## Three things in it worth knowing now

**Email 1 is the whole funnel.** It's the script for asking a roommate to hold the USB key. The activation wall isn't technical — someone who installs the app and never hands the key to anyone has just installed a lock they can open.

**The unsubscribe endpoint must only act on POST.** Scanners and Outlook Safe Links crawl URLs in email. A GET-able one silently unsubscribes real subscribers, and you don't find out until the list shrinks for no reason.

**Complaints are counted, not rated.** Below ~333 Gmail deliveries, one annoyed person is 0.3% and breaches Google's threshold by arithmetic alone. Only 0 and 1 mean anything at your size.

## Ownership

**You:** domain, pricing decision, create the two accounts.
**Me:** everything else — roughly a day, most of it waiting on DNS propagation.
**Coordinate:** the form and confirmation page live in `web/`, which another session owns.

**Buy the domain and I can start Phase 1 immediately.** Everything else in the funnel is downstream of that one purchase.

Here's the full picture.

## Shipped and verified

**The Mac app is a real, downloadable product.** Signed with Developer ID, notarized by Apple (submission `2bd342fa` accepted), stapled, and `spctl` reports `source=Notarized Developer ID`. One command rebuilds it. This needs no Apple approval and could go public today.

**The iPhone blocker is cleared** — the one that had been stuck since June. It turned out to be three separate wrong beliefs:
- The distribution entitlement was **already granted**, since roughly June. Four documents said otherwise and planning was built around a multi-week wait that didn't exist.
- The iOS app had **no real bundle identifier** — it was silently falling back to the wildcard App ID, which can't carry Family Controls. Every shield would have failed.
- `com.sleepwell.ios` belongs to another team, confirmed by a 409 from Apple's API rather than guessed.

Fixed: four App IDs registered under `com.naomiivie.sleepwell`, Family Controls (Distribution) and App Groups on all four, project renamed.

**Billing exists end to end.** Supabase live with schema applied and env vars set. Stripe webhook, entitlement API, Swift client, and the tier gate — all building. Inert until you add Stripe keys.

**Infrastructure recovered.** The repo moved off OneDrive after the file provider dehydrated its git objects. And your **research dossier was rescued** — 195 KB across eight documents that existed only in that dead checkout, staged but never committed, one cache eviction from gone.

## Bugs found that were live

- **The Mac showed the wrong bedtime.** A free user setting 10pm saw "10:00 PM" while the lock actually engaged at midnight — actively misleading them during the exact hours they were relying on it.
- **Hardened runtime missing** on the app target. Would have failed notarization.
- **Devices keyed on display name** — every Mac called "MacBook Air" collapsing into one row, undercounting seats invisibly.
- **Fabricated testimonials were live** on the site — three invented customers and a "used at universities nationwide" claim. Another session caught and removed them.

## Research

Five documents, source-traced rather than aggregated. The dossier now runs sections 1–8 with an index. Notable: three of the most-repeated email statistics **don't survive tracing to a primary source**, and open rate is unusable as a metric because ~57% of opens are machines.

## The honest problem

**Six branches, 100 commits, none merged to `main`.** Three open PRs. `main` is still yesterday morning. That's real sprawl from running four sessions in parallel, and it's the thing I'd fix next.

## Four things wait on you

1. **Buy the domain** — `getsleepwell.com` is available. Blocks the entire funnel; nothing sends without it.
2. **Stripe keys** — backend built and idle.
3. **Disk** — ~10 GB free, TestFlight needs ~16 GB or Xcode Cloud.
4. **The $19.99→$100 renewal** — a 400% increase, exactly what auto-renewal law targets. Your own risk paper cites the authorities. Needs review before anyone is charged.

## GitHub Student Developer Pack

Sign up at [education.github.com/pack](https://education.github.com/pack) with your Wesleyan email. Approval is usually same-day. It includes three separate free-domain offers:

| Provider | What you get |
|---|---|
| **Name.com** | One free domain from 25+ extensions — including **`.app`, `.dev`, `.live`, `.studio`** |
| **Namecheap** | Free `.me` for a year, plus an SSL cert |
| **.TECH** | Free `.tech` for a year |

**Take the Name.com offer and register `trysleepwell.app`.** That gets you the `.app` domain we settled on, free for a year, with WHOIS privacy and an SSL cert included.

**One thing to do immediately after registering:** Name.com requires a card on file (they won't charge for the free year), and **auto-renew is on by default**. Turn it off the moment the domain is registered, or you'll be billed standard price at renewal. Given the product you're building, being auto-renewed into a surprise charge would be a poor joke.

If you'd rather have the bare brand name, the .TECH offer makes `sleepwell.tech` free too — the renewal-cost objection I raised disappears for year one, though it comes back at ~$50 in year two.

## Then

Name.com gives you full DNS control, which is all Phase 1 needs. Point the nameservers at Cloudflare if you want (also free) or just use theirs.

**Tell me the domain once it's registered** and I'll do the six DNS records, DMARC monitoring, and the verification pass. Roughly 30 minutes, and the entire funnel unblocks.

Worth knowing: this also solves it for the Mac download page, so you'd stop shipping from a `*.vercel.app` URL — which is its own small credibility win when you're asking strangers to install something that locks their computer.

## Claude · 2026-08-17T18:18:07.842Z

Nice. Yes — Cloudflare is worth doing, and now is the right moment, before anything depends on the DNS.

**Why:** it's free, the DNS editor is much better than a registrar's, changes propagate in seconds instead of hours, and you won't have to migrate later once records exist. Name.com's DNS would also work — this isn't critical — but you'll be adding six records and editing them a few times, and doing that at Cloudflare is materially less annoying.

**How:** add the site at [dash.cloudflare.com](https://dash.cloudflare.com) → it scans existing records → it gives you two nameservers → paste those into Name.com under DNS/nameserver settings. Propagation is usually under an hour.

**Do not point the domain at Vercel's nameservers as well.** One DNS authority only — two is how records go mysteriously missing.

## Then the order matters

```
1. Cloudflare nameservers          ← you, now
2. Add domain to Vercel project    ← me, via CLI once DNS resolves
3. Create Loops + Resend accounts  ← you (free)
4. Add SPF / DKIM / Return-Path    ← values come FROM step 3
5. Add DMARC, register Postmaster  ← me
6. Verify before any real send     ← me
```

Step 4 is the one people get wrong by doing early — the DKIM and SPF values don't exist until the ESP accounts do, so there's nothing to paste yet.

**What to do right now:** just the Cloudflare nameserver switch. Tell me when it's propagated and I'll attach the domain to the Vercel project, which gets the site and the Mac download onto `trysleepwell.app` instead of a `vercel.app` URL.

Then create the two ESP accounts whenever you have a spare ten minutes, and I'll do the rest of the DNS in one pass.

**The delay is Amazon's, not Cloudflare's.** Loops sends through Amazon SES, so when you hit "Verify records" Loops is really asking SES whether *it* has confirmed the domain. SES polls DNS on its own schedule, and DKIM verification routinely takes anywhere from a few minutes to a few hours even when the records are perfect and global. Loops' "a few hours" message is honest, not boilerplate.

## Claude · 2026-08-17T19:03:18.086Z

Two fields. Fill them in like this:

**Your email address:** whichever inbox you actually read — the weekly digest lands there. Your Gmail is fine.

**Send reports about this domain:** `trysleepwell.app`

Then hit the button and confirm the verification email they send you.

## What comes back

Postmark will give you a DMARC record containing a unique address that looks roughly like:

```
v=DMARC1; p=none; rua=mailto:re+abc123xyz@dmarc.postmarkapp.com;
```

**Don't paste their version wholesale** — send it to me, or use this shape, keeping your token and adding `fo=1`:

```
v=DMARC1; p=none; rua=mailto:re+YOURTOKEN@dmarc.postmarkapp.com; fo=1
```

`fo=1` makes reports fire when *either* SPF or DKIM fails, rather than only when both do. Without it you miss exactly the partial failures worth catching during a monitoring window.

## Then

Edit your existing `_dmarc` TXT record in Cloudflare — replace the Content with that value. Don't add a second record; **two DMARC records on one name is a hard failure**, not a merge. There's only ever one.

Tell me when it's in and I'll verify the syntax and confirm it's live.

After that you're in the two-week monitoring window. Reports start arriving within a day or two, and once they show 100% pass with nothing unexpected, we move to `p=quarantine`, then `p=reject`. That's what stops anyone phishing your users as `hello@trysleepwell.app`.

## Claude · 2026-08-17T19:15:14.501Z

Their template is slightly stale — `pct=` was **removed from the DMARC spec** in May 2026 (RFC 9989 replaced it with `t=`). Harmless, since receivers ignore unknown tags, but there's no reason to publish a tag that no longer exists.

**Use this** — same token, so Postmark's verification still passes:

```
v=DMARC1; p=none; rua=mailto:re+c9djbiw4b6q@dmarc.postmarkapp.com; fo=1
```

Two changes from theirs:

- **Dropped `pct=100` and `aspf=r`** — the first is retired, the second is already the default
- **Added `fo=1`** — reports fire when *either* SPF or DKIM fails, not only when both do. During a monitoring window that's the whole point; without it you'd miss the partial failures worth seeing

I also dropped `sp=none`. It's redundant while `p=none`, since subdomains inherit the parent policy. It becomes load-bearing later — when we move to `p=reject` we'll set `sp=reject` explicitly, or your sending subdomain quietly stays unprotected.

## To apply

Cloudflare → DNS → **Edit** the existing `_dmarc` record → replace Content with the line above → Save. Don't create a second one; two DMARC records on a name is a hard failure rather than a merge.

Tell me when it's saved and I'll verify the syntax and confirm it's serving.

Their "up to 24 hours" is the same story as Loops — both are waiting on external systems polling DNS, not on anything you've done wrong.
