Read it. Now I have what I was missing.

## What you decided in that conversation

**The guarantee**, your wording, now live:

> **We guarantee you'll stop working at 10pm. Or your money back.**
> …if the lock doesn't fire, doesn't cover your screens, or lets you back in early without the key, we'll send you **twice what you paid**.

**And you had the "What it can't do" section removed** — the one listing macOS Recovery, deleting the app during the day, and revocable iPhone permission.

That combination is the thing to fix, and it's fixable without losing any of the punch.

## The problem, precisely

You're now making an **absolute performance claim** while the disclosures that qualified it are gone. Your own `PLATFORM_RISK.md` and architecture docs state the product can be defeated by Recovery mode, a second admin account, or dragging it to the Trash at noon. A headline saying you *guarantee* someone stops working, with the limits page deleted, is the exact shape regulators treat as deceptive under FTC Act §5 — not because the refund is dishonest, but because a reasonable buyer would conclude it can't be beaten.

The other session partly caught this: it moved the exclusion into the guarantee's fine print rather than letting it point at a deleted section. That was the right instinct and it's why this is a fix rather than a fire.

**Second issue: 200% with no verification.** There's no licence check, no account, and a free trial. Anyone can claim the lock failed and you owe $150–200. You've told me you have no money. That's not a legal problem, it's a solvency one.

## The fix — keeps the headline, survives scrutiny

Guarantee what you *control*, in the same confident register:

> **We guarantee you'll stop working at 10pm. Or your money back.**
> If the lock doesn't fire at the time you set, doesn't cover your screens, or lets you back in early without the key — tell us and we'll refund you double.
> *Doesn't cover deleting the app yourself. We told you that before you took your money out.*

Three changes that cost you nothing rhetorically:

1. **Keep one line of limits somewhere before checkout.** Not the whole section — a single sentence. Disclosed-and-brief beats absent.
2. **Cap the double-refund** — "double, up to $200" — and require the trigger to be described. A guarantee with a stated boundary reads as *more* credible, not less.
3. **Timebox it** to 30 days, like Brick and Opal.

---

## Claude · 2026-08-17T19:30:51.765Z

Built and live in Supabase. Six tables now.

## What went in

**`/api/subscribe`** — validates the address, rejects role addresses (`info@`, `abuse@` — those get used as spam traps) and disposable domains, requires an explicit consent tick, stores the consent text plus IP and user agent, then sends the confirmation through Resend. The confirmation carries the download link, so confirming is the step they already wanted rather than a toll.

**`/api/unsubscribe`** — RFC 8058, **POST only**, and it refuses GET explicitly rather than just not implementing it. Scanners and Outlook Safe Links crawl every URL in an email; a GET-able endpoint quietly unsubscribes real people and you don't find out until the list shrinks for no reason.

**Tables** — `subscribers` with double opt-in and opaque tokens, plus `referral_codes` and `referral_redemptions` for the $40/year both-parties deal.

Two small things I made deliberately boring: signing up twice returns the same response as signing up once, and an unknown unsubscribe token returns 200. Otherwise either endpoint becomes a way to test whether a given address is on your list.

Mailing-list signups get `trial_days = 14`, per the offer on the site.

## What's left on the funnel

**Needs you:** add `trysleepwell.app` in Resend and send me the DNS records — the confirmation email can't send until that domain is verified. Same trap as before: **Name is the short prefix, Content is the long target.**

**Needs the web session:** the form component and the `/confirm` page. I've left them clean endpoints to call.

**Needs Stripe:** everything billing — still no account.

The cancellation research is still running; I'll file it and leave it for when you want it.

|---|---|

**If the dashboard says delivered but it's not in your inbox or spam**, check whether Gmail filed it under Promotions — plain-text-looking mail from an unknown sender often lands there.

**If it says bounced**, tell me the error and I'll fix it.

---

Worth saying plainly: **a first send landing in spam is expected, not broken.** You have a domain with no history. The fixes are the ones already planned — the DMARC monitoring window, sending consistently rather than in bursts, and double opt-in so every address genuinely wants the mail. None of that is skippable and none of it works instantly.

It's also why the funnel plan puts a **reply-based** activation metric first: a reply from a real person is the strongest signal you can send Gmail that your mail is wanted.

Tell me what the dashboard says and I'll take it from there.

The headers are a clean sweep — this is the best possible result:

```
dkim=pass   header.i=@trysleepwell.app   ← your domain, not Amazon's
spf=pass    smtp.mailfrom=send.trysleepwell.app
dmarc=pass  header.from=trysleepwell.app
```

DKIM signed by **your** domain means you build your own reputation rather than borrowing Amazon's. That's the whole point of the DNS work, and it's correct.

One thing: the label on that message reads **Inbox**, not Promotions. If you found it under the Promotions tab, tell me and I'll treat it differently — but Gmail's own chip says otherwise.

## On the address

`naomi@trysleepwell.app` from "Naomi" is actually the highest-performing pattern for this kind of product — Superhuman, Buffer and Clearbit all send founder mail exactly this way, and a person's name beats a role address on both opens and replies.

If it feels wrong, the alternatives are `hello@trysleepwell.app` (company voice, less personal) or `naomi@` with a display name of "Naomi from Sleep Well" (clearer at a glance in a crowded inbox). I'd take that last one — it keeps the person and adds the context.

**Which do you want?** It's one line to change.

## On the email looking unprofessional

That was a bare diagnostic — no formatting, no signature, no design, written to be read by me in raw headers. It isn't representative of anything.

The real emails are already drafted in the funnel plan with proper subject lines, preheaders, and structure. The design decisions are made too: single column, 600px, 16px type, live text, midtones that survive dark mode.

**The one thing I'd push back on:** the research is fairly clear that for a founder-led product, mail that looks *plain* outperforms mail that looks *designed* — HubSpot's tests found heavy HTML got 51% fewer clicks. "Professional" here means well-written and well-structured, not visually branded. A newsletter-looking template would read as marketing and land in Promotions far more reliably than this test did.

## Why it went to Promotions, and what actually fixes it

Not one big thing — Gmail scores a sender it has never seen before, and my test message was working against itself:

- **Three URLs in a short email.** Link density is one of the strongest Promotions signals, and my test had `trysleepwell.app`, `amazonses.com` and another all auto-linked. The new one has zero links.
- **No sending history.** Your domain is hours old. Gmail has nothing to go on and defaults to caution.
- **No prior interaction.** You've never replied to or opened mail from this sender.

**The two things that fix it are both yours, and they beat anything I can change in the code:**

1. **Drag it from Promotions to Primary.** Gmail learns per-sender — one move teaches it where the next one belongs.
2. **Hit reply and send anything.** A reply is the single strongest signal Gmail accepts that a sender is wanted.

I put both requests in the email itself so it's one action, not two.

Worth knowing this is also why the funnel plan uses **replies** as the activation metric rather than opens — it does double duty as a deliverability signal on a cold domain.

## The honest split on Promotions

3. **Consistent sending** over weeks
4. Low link density and plain formatting

That's why the sequence asks for a reply in the first two emails. It's not a stylistic tic — it's the only lever that works on a cold domain.

## Claude · 2026-08-17T20:09:26.692Z

**IAP = In-App Purchase.** Apple's payment system, built into the App Store.

**The cut:** 30% standard, but **15% for you** under the Small Business Program — that's automatic for anyone under $1M/year, which is everyone starting out. You apply once and it applies to all sales.

So on a $100/year subscription: Apple takes $15, you keep $85. And it drops further — **after a subscriber has been with you 12 months, it falls to 15% anyway** under the standard rules, so there's no step-up later.

Compare: **Stripe takes ~2.9% + 30¢** — about $3.20 on that same $100.

## But here's the part that decides it

**IAP only works for App Store apps.** Your Mac app ships via Developer ID as a direct download — it can't use IAP at all. And it can't move to the Mac App Store, because that requires sandboxing, which would kill the pf firewall, the root LaunchDaemon, and the watchdog. The enforcement stack *is* the product.

So it splits:

| | Can use IAP? |
|---|---|
| **Mac** ($75/yr) | **No.** Developer ID only — needs Stripe |
| **iPhone/iPad** ($100/yr all-devices) | Yes |

Going IAP for iOS means running **two billing systems** and reconciling them — someone who pays on iPhone shouldn't pay again on Mac, which means mapping an Apple transaction to the same account my `subscriptions` table keys on.

## What's already here

There's a script on `main` from a Cursor agent that creates four IAP products via the App Store Connect API — and I already have working API credentials from this afternoon, so I could run it.

**But the products it creates don't match your pricing** — it defines "Complete, Family, Pro Monthly, Pro Annual," which predates the tiers you settled ($75 Mac, $100 all-devices, $199 family, $399 lifetime, $29 student).

**Before I touch it:** IAP for iOS *in addition to* Stripe for Mac, or are you thinking IAP instead of Stripe entirely? The second doesn't work — the Mac app has no way to take money through Apple.

## Claude · 2026-08-17T20:13:06.276Z

You did look at it, yes. And it's arguably the better fit for you — for one reason that matters more than the fee.

## Merchant of Record

Lemon Squeezy is the **seller of record**, not you. They collect and remit sales tax and VAT worldwide. You never register for VAT in the EU, never track US state nexus, never file anything.

With Stripe, **you** are the merchant. Selling to a customer in Germany makes you responsible for German VAT. That's a real administrative burden for a solo founder, and given your CPT/immigration situation, "fewer tax entities in my name" has value beyond the money.

| | Lemon Squeezy | Stripe |
|---|---|---|
| Fee | **5% + 50¢** | 2.9% + 30¢ |
| On $100 | you keep $94.50 | you keep $96.80 |
| Sales tax / VAT | **they handle it** | yours |
| Licence keys | **built in** | build it yourself |

That second row is the one to weigh. **$2.30 per sale** is what you're paying for someone else to own global tax compliance and generate licence keys.

And yes — Stripe acquired Lemon Squeezy in 2024. It still runs as its own product with its own pricing; the acquisition doesn't change what you get.

## The licence-key part matters here

Your Mac app ships outside the App Store, so it can't use IAP and needs some way to activate a paid copy. Lemon Squeezy generates and validates licence keys natively. With Stripe I'd be building that from scratch — it's the piece I flagged days ago as "days, not hours."

## What it costs me to switch

My webhook is Stripe-shaped. Lemon Squeezy's events differ, so `/api/stripe/webhook` becomes `/api/lemonsqueezy/webhook` and the handler is rewritten. The `subscriptions` table, entitlement API and Swift client are all unaffected — they don't care who took the money.

Maybe two hours.

## My read

**Lemon Squeezy for Mac** (MoR + licence keys), **IAP for iOS** (Apple requires it for App Store apps anyway). Two systems either way, but each is doing the thing it's actually best at, and you own no tax obligations.

Want me to rebuild the webhook for Lemon Squeezy?

## Naomi · 2026-08-17T20:14:54.802Z

why cant lemon squeezy also work for iphone

## Claude · 2026-08-17T20:15:15.143Z

It can, technically — but Apple won't let you use it inside the app.

**App Store rules:** if you sell a subscription that unlocks functionality in an iOS app, Apple requires In-App Purchase. Taking payment through Lemon Squeezy *inside* the app gets you rejected in review, or removed if you slip through.

There is one legitimate route: **since the May 2025 Epic ruling, US-storefront apps can link out to external checkout.** So the iPhone app could show a "Subscribe on the web" link that opens Lemon Squeezy in Safari. That's allowed now, where it wasn't two years ago.

Two catches:

- **Apple still takes 15%** of anything bought within 7 days of tapping that link. So on iOS-referred sales you'd pay **15% (Apple) + 5% (Lemon Squeezy) = 20%** — worse than IAP alone.
- **US storefront only.** Outside the US you need an entitlement that isn't available in most markets, so international iPhone users would have no way to pay.

## Where that leaves it

| Route | Cut | Works? |
|---|---|---|
| **IAP on iOS** | 15% | Yes, everywhere |
| **Lemon Squeezy via in-app link** | 15% + 5% | US only, worse economics |
| **Lemon Squeezy from your website** | 5% | Yes — and Apple gets nothing |

That third row is the interesting one. **If someone buys on your website without going through the app, Apple has no claim at all.** Your funnel drives people to `trysleepwell.app`, they subscribe there, then sign in on their phone. That's entirely legitimate and it's what Spotify and Netflix do — the app just can't *link* to it.

So the honest answer: **Lemon Squeezy can serve iPhone users, as long as they buy on the web rather than in the app.** IAP is only needed if you want people to be able to subscribe from inside the app itself.

Given your funnel already pushes traffic to the website, you could reasonably start Lemon Squeezy only, add IAP later if you find iPhone users won't leave the app to pay.

**That would be one payment system instead of two.** Want me to build it that way?

## Naomi · 2026-08-17T20:19:06.347Z

but is it easier for people to spend the money if they just use IAP and apple automatically charges them? whats our cut then

## Claude · 2026-08-17T20:19:34.572Z

Yes, IAP converts better — but less than you'd think, and there's a way to get most of the benefit for a third of the cost.

## The cut

| | You keep on $100 |
|---|---|
| **IAP** (15%, Small Business Program) | **$85.00** |
| **Lemon Squeezy** (5% + 50¢) | **$94.50** |

**$9.50 a sale.** For IAP to be worth it, it has to convert **more than ~11% better** than web checkout.

## Why IAP converts better

One reason, and it's a good one: **no card entry.** Face ID, done. Card entry on a phone is the single biggest drop-off point in any mobile checkout — people abandon at the keyboard.

## But Lemon Squeezy checkout supports Apple Pay

Which means on an iPhone their web checkout is *also* Face ID and one tap. Same gesture, same absence of typing — for 5% instead of 15%.

That closes most of the gap. The remaining friction is leaving the app to a browser, which is real but small compared to typing a card number.

## The thing that decides it for you

**Your main product is the Mac app.** Someone downloading Mac software is sitting at a Mac, where entering a card is completely normal and IAP isn't even available. The iPhone side is the add-on, and your funnel drives people to the website anyway.

So IAP would be optimising the *smaller* half of your revenue, at triple the fee, while adding a second billing system to reconcile.

## What I'd do

**Lemon Squeezy only, with Apple Pay enabled.** One system, 5%, no tax obligations, licence keys included, and iPhone buyers still get Face ID checkout.

Revisit IAP if you later see people bouncing off the "subscribe on the web" link — that's a measurable thing, and you can add IAP then without unpicking anything.

**Want me to build Lemon Squeezy?** It's the webhook rewrite plus licence-key handling, roughly two hours, and it replaces the Stripe path entirely.

## Claude · 2026-08-17T20:22:19.915Z

Email work is in. Here's what changed and why the numbers aren't arbitrary:

**16px body, 1.6 line-height** — that's the accessibility floor, and below 14px the Gmail iOS app inflates fonts by 50% and wrecks the layout.

**Single column, 600px, no media queries** — Gmail strips `<style>` blocks for non-Google accounts, which silently deletes every media query. A single column needs none.

**Midtone colours, no pure black or white** — Gmail iOS and Outlook fully invert in dark mode, and the extremes get mangled.

**Still one link, still no `List-Unsubscribe`.** Those two are why it reached Primary. I left a comment where someone would be tempted to add a styled CTA button, explaining that doing so moves it to Promotions.

**On the avatar** — I didn't attempt it. Gmail only shows one from a Google Workspace profile or BIMI, and BIMI needs a VMC certificate requiring a registered trademark at four figures a year. That's a later-stage thing.

If Apple Pay on Lemon Squeezy converts close to IAP, the answer is no and you run one system at 5%. That's what I've asked the research to settle.

<result>## Bottom line up front

Two of the premises behind this question don't survive contact with the data:

1. **Desktop is the *better*-converting surface, not the worse one.** Every measured dataset shows desktop converting ~1.6–1.9× mobile. If you're worried about a device-driven conversion problem, the loss is on mobile.
2. **Apple Pay on desktop is no longer Safari-only.** Since iOS 18 (public rollout ~24 Feb 2025), Apple Pay on the Web renders in Chrome/Edge/Firefox on Mac, Windows and ChromeOS via a QR code scanned with an iPhone. That changes the "eligible population" math in Q4 substantially — *but* Stripe's own docs still don't mention this flow (details below).

And the central question — **how much desktop drop-off is caused by manual card entry** — has **no clean public answer**. No study I could find isolates it. Everything circulating is either vendor marketing with undisclosed methodology or SEO-blog recycling.

---

## 1. Desktop vs mobile conversion benchmarks

### Contentsquare 2026 Digital Experience Benchmark — strongest available source
- **Sample:** 99 billion web and app sessions, 6,500+ websites, 9 industries, Q4 2024 vs Q4 2025
- **Published:** 25 Feb 2026 (report); conversions section dated 9 Mar 2026
- **Classification:** Vendor-published but **genuinely measured panel data** from analytics installs. Contentsquare sells experience analytics, not payments or device optimization — no commercial stake in the desktop/mobile answer. Treat as the closest thing to independent.

| Metric | Value |
|---|---|
| Desktop CVR vs mobile | **74% higher** (stated verbatim on their page) |
| All-industry | ~3.4% desktop vs ~2.0% mobile |
| Retail | **3.7% desktop vs 2.0% mobile** (confirmed on their retail page, cited to the 2026 report) |
| Travel &amp; hospitality | 4.5% desktop vs 2.1% mobile |
| Mobile traffic share | 69.9% of all sessions |
| YoY change | Desktop CVR **−7.9%**, mobile −3.5% (retail: desktop −8%) |

Caveat: the 3.7%/2.0% retail pair is directly sourced on their page; the ~3.4%/2.0% all-industry pair I only got via search snippet, not the primary page — the "74% higher" ratio is the number I'd quote. Contentsquare defines conversion as "share of visits that include a conversion event, like a purchase or form submission" — i.e. **not purely purchases**, which matters.

**2025 edition** (published 28 Jan 2025; 90B sessions, 6,000 sites, 389B page views, Q4'23 vs Q4'24): overall CVR down 6.1% YoY, cost per visit up 9%. The free press release carries **no device split**.

### Littledata (Shopify)
- Desktop **1.9%** vs mobile **1.2%** (desktop ~58% higher); overall Shopify average 1.4%
- **Sample:** 2,800 ecommerce sites; **2023 data**, still being republished as current
- **Classification:** Vendor (analytics), measured. Physical-goods Shopify stores — poor proxy for software.

### Adobe Analytics / Digital Economy Index
- Mobile = **51.4%** of US online spend, Oct 2025; **56.4%** of 2025 holiday-season transactions on smartphone (up from 54.5% in 2024)
- **Classification:** Independent measured (Adobe Analytics, ~1T visits to US retail sites). **Adobe does not publish free conversion rate by device** — only spend/transaction share. Don't let anyone tell you otherwise.

### Not verified
Salesforce Shopping Index device splits are widely quoted (~76% of visits, ~two-thirds of orders from mobile). The Statista page hosting it returned a redirect loop; I could not verify against primary. **Flagging as unconfirmed** rather than reporting it.

---

## 2. SaaS / software / digital downloads specifically

**There is no public data.** This is the honest answer.

I searched for device-split transaction data from Paddle, FastSpring, Lemon Squeezy, Gumroad, ChartMogul, Baremetrics and ProfitWell/Paddle Studios. **None of them publish it.** Their public content is exclusively fee comparisons, MoR positioning and MRR/churn benchmarks. No device dimension anywhere.

Two things worth knowing:

- **Contentsquare's 2026 benchmark does include "Software" as one of its 9 industries** — so a software-vertical device split exists in their dataset. It is not in the free content; it's behind the gated report / Benchmark Explorer. **This is the single highest-value thing to go get.** Downloading the gated 2026 report would likely answer your actual question.
- **The "70%+ of B2B SaaS paid conversions happen on desktop even when 50%+ of traffic is mobile" figure is untraceable.** It appears across several 2026 SEO roundups with no primary citation. I could not find an origin. **Discard it.** Same for "50.48% of SaaS conversions are mobile" — same class of unsourced roundup, and it contradicts the first one, which tells you what both are worth.

Contentsquare published a standalone *2021 B2B Digital Experience Benchmark* PDF that likely contains B2B device splits; the file returned unparseable binary on fetch. Recoverable if you want it, but it's 5 years stale.

---

## 3. Card-entry friction on desktop

### The direct answer: nobody has measured this

**Baymard's abandonment-reasons list has no "typing card details was too annoying" category at all.** Reasons, excluding "just browsing" abandoners:

| Reason | % |
|---|---|
| Extra costs too high (shipping, tax, fees) | 40% |
| Delivery too slow | 20% |
| Didn't trust site with credit card info | 19% |
| Site wanted me to create an account | 18% |
| Too long / complicated checkout process | 17% |
| Website had errors / crashed | 17% |
| Returns policy unsatisfactory | 13% |
| Couldn't see total cost up front | 12% |
| Credit card declined | 10% |
| Not enough payment methods | 9% |
| Don't know | 7% |

- **Overall documented cart abandonment: 70.22%**, averaged across **50 studies, 2006–2025**, page last updated 22 Sep 2025. **Independent.**
- **Methodology gap:** Baymard's free page does **not** disclose respondent count, survey date or country for the reasons survey. Third-party blogs report "n=4,329 US adults (2024)" and "n=4,384" — two different numbers, neither traceable to the primary page. **Treat n as not disclosed.**
- Multi-select, so percentages exceed 100%.
- **No desktop/mobile breakdown is published** for either the rate or the reasons.

The closest Baymard gets is qualitative: the card number is "by far the most complex and error-prone input during checkout," a single typo triggers validation failure, and **34% of sites wipe card fields after a validation error** — which they observed directly causing abandonments in usability testing. Real, but it's a design-defect finding, not a conversion percentage, and not desktop-specific.

### Wallet-lift claims — all vendor marketing

| Claim | Source | Methodology disclosed | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| Apple Pay: **+22.3% conversion, +22.5% revenue** | Stripe blog, 10 Apr 2025 | "Statistically significant holdback experiment"; $1.4T annual volume as context. **No sample size, no date range, no significance threshold, no device split.** | **VENDOR MARKETING.** Stripe sells Apple Pay acceptance. |
| Stripe Link: **+14% conversion** for businesses with large returning-customer bases | Stripe docs | None | **VENDOR MARKETING** |
| Payment Element: **+10.5% revenue** | Stripe, 28 Apr 2023 | 5,000 migrating businesses vs 5,000 matched controls on business size/type/industry/geography. Stripe itself notes uplift "may include the impact of other unobserved changes." | **VENDOR MARKETING, but methodology disclosed.** Observational, not randomized. No device split. |
| "Apple Pay lifts desktop conversions ≥15%" | Nomupay blog, citing unnamed analyses of CocoWeb/Lululemon/J.Crew | None | **Discard.** No traceable primary. |
| Shop Pay "+50% vs guest checkout" | Shopify | Commissioned | **VENDOR MARKETING** |

**Counter-evidence worth weighing:** Fundraise Up ran their own Stripe Link A/B test and found **just over 1% of donors chose it, with minimal revenue impact** — a real third-party test producing far less than Stripe's numbers. Different context (nonprofit donations), but it's the only non-vendor test I found and it points the other way.

### The one independent study
**"Fewer Clicks, More Purchases," Unal &amp; Park, *Management Science*, published 22 Feb 2023 (Cornell).** One-click-checkout adopters spent **+28.5%**, purchased **43% more often**, bought **36% more items**, visited 7% more.

**Do not cite this as a checkout conversion lift.** It compares customers who *chose to adopt* one-click against their own prior behavior — self-selection is uncontrolled, it's a single retailer, and it measures spending/frequency, not checkout completion rate. No device split.

### On autofill specifically
No published data on credit-card autofill adoption rates or manual-vs-autofill completion rates. I checked eBay's engineering deep-dive on browser autofill (timed out on fetch, may be worth a retry) and Chrome/Firefox documentation — the browser vendors document the feature but publish no usage or conversion statistics.

---

## 4. macOS Safari share of desktop — and why the framing is out of date

### StatCounter desktop browser share, July 2026

| Browser | Worldwide | United States |
|---|---|---|
| Chrome | 70.58% | 59.90% |
| Edge | 11.15% | 12.86% |
| Firefox | 6.51% | 11.66% |
| **Safari** | **5.66%** | **11.24%** |
| Opera | 2.00% | — |

Cross-checked: a second 2026 analysis of StatCounter puts desktop Safari at 5.29% worldwide, consistent with 5.66%. **Confidence: good.**

**Do not trust my desktop-OS-share reads.** StatCounter's OS view returned an implausible parse (separate "OS X" and "macOS" rows, Linux at 11.92% in the US). Browser share is the correct denominator here anyway, since Apple Pay eligibility historically keyed off Safari, not off macOS.

### The premise has changed
Apple's own Safari User Guide now carries a page titled **"Pay with Apple Pay in third-party browsers"**: you can use Apple Pay "on your Mac, Windows devices, and other devices in third-party browsers" by scanning an on-screen **QR code with an iPhone or iPad** and confirming with Face ID / Touch ID / passcode. Adyen and BigCommerce both announced support effective **24 Feb 2025**, requiring iOS 18 on the phone and only the latest Apple Pay JS SDK on the merchant side.

**In Safari on a Mac** the older constraints still hold: complete the purchase with **Touch ID on the Mac**, or a **paired iPhone (iOS 10+) or Apple Watch (watchOS 3+) signed into the same Apple Account**. Apple notes **only one user account on a Mac can use Mac Touch ID** for Apple Pay; others must use iPhone/Watch.

### The Stripe caveat — this is the practically important part
**Stripe's Apple Pay documentation, checked today, still reads: "Stripe users can accept Apple Pay… on the web in Safari starting with iOS 10 or macOS Sierra."** There is **no mention** of third-party desktop browsers or the QR flow anywhere in their web integration guide. Stripe also notes that Checkout in `embedded_page` ui_mode supports **only Safari 17+**.

So: **if you're on Stripe, assume desktop Apple Pay ≈ Safari only in practice**, giving you roughly **11% of US desktop traffic** as the eligible ceiling — and less than that after subtracting Safari users without Touch ID, a paired device, or a card in Wallet. Nobody publishes that final eligible-share number. Worth confirming directly with Stripe support before building a plan around desktop Apple Pay.

---

## What I could not find, stated plainly

- Any device split for SaaS/software/digital-download purchases from any merchant of record or subscription-analytics vendor
- Any study attributing a measurable share of **desktop** abandonment to manual card entry
- Any A/B test of wallets that reports desktop and mobile results separately
- Any published card-autofill adoption rate
- Conversion rate by device for Contentsquare's "Software" industry (exists in their data, gated)

The gap between "desktop converts 74% better than mobile" and "manual card entry is killing desktop conversion" is not bridged by any evidence I could locate. If a plan depends on that bridge, the cheapest way to close it is your own funnel instrumentation — step-level drop-off at the card form, segmented by device — because the public record won't do it for you.

## Claude · 2026-08-17T20:35:26.221Z

A sub-agent from that research finished before I stopped it, and it overturns two things I told you.

**Desktop converts 74% *better* than mobile** — not worse (Contentsquare, 99 billion sessions). Your primary product is the Mac app, sold to people sitting at a desktop. That's the strongest-converting surface there is, and IAP can't touch it anyway.

**The Apple Pay conversion claims are vendor marketing.** Stripe's "+22.3% from Apple Pay" discloses no sample size, no date range, no device split — and Stripe sells Apple Pay acceptance. The one independent test found just over 1% of users chose the wallet option with minimal revenue impact.

So when I said Apple Pay "closes most of the gap," I was repeating a number I shouldn't have trusted. The honest position is that **nobody has published a clean comparison** of IAP versus web checkout conversion, and the friction argument for IAP rests on mobile card entry — which isn't where your revenue is.

**Net: the Lemon Squeezy decision holds, on firmer ground than when you made it.** One system, 5%, no tax obligations, licence keys included, serving the desktop buyers who convert best.

- **The legal entity is now "Sold through Link, LLC f/k/a Lemon Squeezy LLC"**

So I spent the last hour building a webhook for a product in wind-down, and recommended it to you as the one system to run everything through. That was wrong, and I should have checked whether it was still accepting customers before writing code against it.

## Also: the successor costs more than I said

Stripe Managed Payments is widely reported as "5% + 50¢, same as Lemon Squeezy." It isn't. Stripe's own pricing page says **3.5% *in addition to* Payments fees** — so roughly **6.4% + 30¢** US domestic.

## Real options

| | Fee | Merchant of record | Licence keys |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Paddle** | ~5% + 50¢ | yes | yes |
| **Polar** | 5% + 50¢, drops to 3.8% at $20/mo | yes | yes |
| **Stripe Managed Payments** | ~6.4% + 30¢ | yes | no |
| Plain Stripe | 2.9% + 30¢ | **no — you owe VAT** | no |

**Paddle or Polar.** Both are merchant of record, both do licence keys, both are actually accepting customers. Polar is cheaper and newer; Paddle is established and has been doing this for over a decade.

The webhook I built is ~80% reusable — the signature verification and status mapping change, the table and entitlement logic don't. Maybe an hour.

**Which one?** I'd take Paddle for a product you're asking strangers to trust with money, but Polar's pricing is genuinely better and I don't want to talk you out of it on vibes.

## Setup — 15 minutes

1. **Create your store**, currency USD
2. **Products:**
   - Sleep Well Mac — **$75/year** subscription
   - Sleep Well All Devices — **$100/year** subscription
   - Family — **$199/year** subscription
   - Family Lifetime — **$399** one-time
3. **Settings → Payment methods** → enable **Apple Pay** and **PayPal**
4. **Settings → Webhooks → +**
   - URL: `https://trysleepwell.app/api/lemonsqueezy/webhook`
   - Events: `subscription_created`, `subscription_updated`, `subscription_cancelled`, `subscription_resumed`, `subscription_expired`, `subscription_payment_failed`
   - Copy the **signing secret**

**Send me the signing secret** and I'll put it in Vercel. Then a test purchase should land a row in Supabase, and billing is live end to end.

**One thing to know when you build the checkout:** Apple Pay works on Lemon Squeezy's **hosted checkout page**, not the embedded overlay — a known limitation on their side. Use the hosted page and it works; embed it and the Apple Pay button silently doesn't appear.

## Naomi · 2026-08-17T20:39:13.764Z

it requries a passport to sign up - which mine is experied

## Claude · 2026-08-17T20:39:40.894Z

Straight answer: **use Stripe, with your SSN.**

Merchant-of-record providers require a passport because they're the legal seller — they carry the compliance burden, so their KYC is strict and they won't budge. That's Lemon Squeezy, Paddle and Polar all the same.

**Stripe isn't a merchant of record**, so its verification is lighter. For a US-based individual it typically wants your **SSN and address**, not a passport. If you have an SSN from your CPT authorization — which you should — that's very likely enough.

**The trade-off, honestly:** Stripe means *you* are the merchant, so you're responsible for sales tax and VAT. At zero customers that's theoretical. It becomes real somewhere around your first few hundred international sales, and you migrate to an MoR then — with a renewed passport and actual revenue making it worth the effort.

**Fee is also better:** 2.9% + 30¢ versus 5% + 50¢.

## What that costs me

Nothing. **The Stripe webhook is already built** — `/api/stripe/webhook`, written days ago, tested, sitting in the repo unused. I never deleted it.

So:

1. [dashboard.stripe.com/register](https://dashboard.stripe.com/register) — sign up with your SSN
2. Create the four products
3. Webhook → `https://trysleepwell.app/api/stripe/webhook`
4. Send me the secret key and signing secret

If Stripe also demands a passport, tell me and we'll look at Gumroad — much lighter verification, but 10% flat.

**Separately, and not a Sleep Well problem:** renew the passport. An expired one will block more than payment processors, and it takes weeks.
